# Review: Vimy Ridge, 9-12 April 1917: Canadian Corps (draft)

Status: `draft`. Generated by `sim review`; do not edit by hand.

## Summary

- Values: 1681
- Sourced: 417
- Estimates: 1255
- Disputed: 9
- By source tier (sourced values; a disputed value counts under its best source): primary 29, official 222, scholarly 0, secondary 87, tertiary 88

Estimates are listed first in each section. Leader ratings are the builder's drafts and are yours to change. Doctrine and model-table rows give each parameter's meaning after its value; "calibrated (Plan 4)" marks a model coefficient that is fitted, not looked up.

| **objectives / black-4cdn barrage-4cdn / earliest**: 1917-04-09T18:45 | estimate | estimate | a lower bound, not a reported capture time: BOH p. 341 has the 102nd Battalion hold its half from the first rush, the 87th's front trench taken by bombers after 1 p.m., and two companies of the 85th, who completed the line, 'started off across No Man's Land at 6.45 p.m.'; 18:45 is the start of that attack, so the line cannot have fallen before it (historical.md) |
| **objectives / red-3cdn barrage-3cdn / earliest**: 1917-04-09T07:05 | estimate | estimate | a lower bound from the timetable, not a reported capture time: BOH p. 304 has the barrage reach the 3rd and 4th Divisions' final objectives 'at Zero plus 95 minutes'; the line cannot have fallen before the barrage lifted onto it, and no earlier capture is recorded |

## Leaders and ratings

| Value | How it is known | Source | Note |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **byng / planning**: 5 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. Nicholson p. 247: wrote the corps 'Scheme of Operations' (5 March); p. 250: a full-scale replica of the battle area laid out 'under General Byng's personal direction', rehearsed from platoon to division |
| **byng / orderSpeed**: 4 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. pp. 257-258: 'shortly after two o'clock' ordered a squadron to push on to Willerval, and at 2:40 telephoned First Army to propose using cavalry, once the Brown Line 'seemed assured' |
| **byng / adaptiveness**: 3 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. p. 258: sought to exploit beyond the plan the same afternoon; but p. 268 calls the corps timetable 'inflexible', and the cavalry had been left out of the scheme (p. 258) |
| **byng / riskTolerance**: 3 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. p. 258: the attack was framed as a limited-objective, rigidly timed set-piece; the afternoon cavalry proposal was the one reach beyond it |
| **byng / moraleEffect**: 4 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. p. 250: commanders 'at all levels freely briefed their subordinates' so that 'all ranks knew just where they would have to go'; no direct evidence of his personal effect on morale |
| **currie / planning**: 5 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. p. 250: studied the French Verdun attack and lectured the corps on its lessons; p. 258: his division took all four objectives 'on schedule' |
| **currie / orderSpeed**: 4 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. p. 255: when the Red Line's right 500 yards held out, the 1st Brigade was brought forward from reserve to take it as part of the next bound |
| **currie / adaptiveness**: 4 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. p. 255: re-tasked the reserve brigade to the uncaptured right of the Red Line; p. 257: threw back the right flank to keep touch with the 51st Division |
| **currie / riskTolerance**: 3 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. p. 247-248: kept to the corps timetable and its pauses; nothing in the sources read shows him reaching beyond it |
| **currie / moraleEffect**: 3 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. no conduct at Vimy found in the sources read; the rubric midpoint until the owner rates it |
| **burstall / planning**: 4 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. p. 254: Black Line by 6:15; p. 255: Red by eight o'clock; p. 256: Blue, then p. 257 Brown, with the attached British 13th Brigade worked into the plan on the widening front |
| **burstall / orderSpeed**: 3 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. no conduct at Vimy found in the sources read; the rubric midpoint until the owner rates it |
| **burstall / adaptiveness**: 3 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. p. 254: the eight attached tanks failed early and the attack went on without them; the plan had not relied on them (p. 248) |
| **burstall / riskTolerance**: 3 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. no conduct at Vimy found in the sources read; the rubric midpoint until the owner rates it |
| **burstall / moraleEffect**: 3 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. no conduct at Vimy found in the sources read; the rubric midpoint until the owner rates it |
| **lipsett / planning**: 4 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. p. 254: 'in general the attack went like clockwork'; Black confirmed by 6:25 and the crest by 7:30 (p. 255) |
| **lipsett / orderSpeed**: 4 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. p. 256: at about nine o'clock, on learning Hill 145 was still German, the 7th Brigade was set to form a defensive flank |
| **lipsett / adaptiveness**: 4 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. p. 256: the defensive flank on the open left, from the Bois de la Folie back to the old front line |

| **falkenhausen / planning**: 1 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. Nicholson p. 267: blamed 'for having misjudged the strength and the frontage of the expected attack'; p. 268 and BOH p. 354: held the counter-attack divisions 15-20 miles back although Ludendorff and Rupprecht urged them closer; BOH p. 353: the defence-in-depth programme 'hardly got beyond the paper stage'. Nicholson p. 268 adds that the Army was 'criticized beyond its just deserts' |
| **falkenhausen / orderSpeed**: 2 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. Nicholson p. 268: 'in no hurry to commit these divisions, two of which, held near Douai, might have reached Vimy Ridge by rail in the first four hours'; BOH p. 354: at dawn on 9 April their orders 'remained unchanged, with the exception of those of a few battalions' |
| **falkenhausen / adaptiveness**: 2 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. Nicholson p. 260: 'not realizing the extent of the Canadian Corps' success', still treated the reserve divisions 'primarily as a relief'; BOH p. 354: met the attack on the Somme pattern, sending resting and Army-reserve battalions to block the flanks of a break-through |
| **falkenhausen / riskTolerance**: 3 | estimate | estimate | owner to confirm. BOH p. 241: kept the counter-attack divisions 12-24 hours' march back 'so as to avoid the risk of their being shelled'; but BOH p. 355: on 10 April decided to counter-attack through Farbus and was overruled by Rupprecht. The two pull against each other, so the midpoint |

## German order of battle and garrisons

| Value | How it is known | Source | Note |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **I./RIR 261 / 1./RIR 261 / strength**: 125 | estimate | estimate | BOH 1917 I p. 317: the forward two battalions of the five regiments facing the Corps 'at most 5,000 men' = 125 a company (40 companies); rifle strength 'seventy or eighty' (same page; Nicholson p. 247 'about 75'); the owner's brief has about 150 (oob-german.md, Strength) |
| **I./RIR 261 / 1./RIR 261 / machineGuns**: 2.25 | estimate | estimate | the regiment's three machine-gun companies, one attached to each battalion (german-handbook-1917 p. 49), of 6 guns each (p. 50): 18 guns over the regiment's 8 companies in the line (its third battalion rested off the map); 1.5 if the resting battalion's company rested with it (bombardment.md, Garrisons) |
| **I./RIR 261 / 1./RIR 261 / dugoutDepthM**: 9.1 | estimate | estimate | 30 feet: BOH p. 316 puts the main German dug-outs and tunnels 'twenty to fifty feet down in the chalk' (6.1-15.2 m), p. 315 deep dug-outs 'about 120 yards apart in the first and second trenches'; these were the older 'deep mined dug-outs' of the front system (p. 353), so the lower half of the range (bombardment.md, Garrisons) |

| **I./RIR 263 / 4./RIR 263 / dugoutDepthM**: 9.1 | estimate | estimate | 30 feet: BOH p. 316 puts the main German dug-outs and tunnels 'twenty to fifty feet down in the chalk' (6.1-15.2 m), p. 315 deep dug-outs 'about 120 yards apart in the first and second trenches'; these were the older 'deep mined dug-outs' of the front system (p. 353), so the lower half of the range (bombardment.md, Garrisons) |

Weapon: BOH gives no split of the 89, so all are entered as the representative 150mm-sfh, the most numerous heavy class on the Arras front (148 of 355 heavy pieces were 15-cm to 19.9-cm howitzers, BOH p. 182 n.1); an estimate (fireplan.md, Weapon ids) |

Weapon: BOH gives no split of the 108, so all are entered as the representative 77mm-fk (419 of the Arras front's 659 field pieces were 77-mm and 9-cm guns, the rest 105-mm howitzers, BOH p. 182 n.1); an estimate (fireplan.md, Weapon ids) |

Nicholson counts guns, BOH battery positions. The '85% of 175 located' lead (plan, from the owner's brief) was not found in any source read (fireplan.md, Open questions) |

Some were wire-cutting with the 106 fuze (BOH p. 313), not split out |

| **spared / ger-first-position / reason**: A stretch of German trench (a strong point and its wire) was left undestroyed by the heavy artillery at the request of the 87th Battalion's commanding officer, who meant to use it when captured; its machine guns cut down half the 87th's leading wave | sourced | nicholson-1962, 259 (official) | 'A portion of German trench had been left undestroyed by the heavy artillery at the request of the commanding officer' of the 87th. BOH p. 328: 'The heavy artillery commander had desired to destroy it again', but at the infantry's request 'it was allowed to remain'; it had been destroyed early in the bombardment and repaired by the 7th, with its covering wire, which then 'prevented all attempts to rush the strong point' (bombardment.md, The spared stretch) |

## Command, flanks and end conditions

| Value | How it is known | Source | Note |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **fire / xvii-corps-right-flank allied 1917-04-09T05:30–1917-04-09T13:18 / guns**: 23 | estimate | estimate | no source read says how many XVII Corps guns fired on the Canadian flank. Its heavy artillery had ten 60-pdr and two 6-inch gun batteries (BOH p. 226); at the Canadian Corps' sizes (54 60-pdrs in nine batteries, BOH p. 306 n.1 and rca-ww1-history; 8 6-inch guns in two) that is 68 long guns, and one-third, the share of its left division of three, is 23. An upper-end guess: the guns behind the 51st Division served its own attack, and how many of them turned on Farbus Wood is not known; App. 29 para. 1 has the XVII Corps 'with its left on Farbus Wood' (command.md) |
| **attacks / British 24th Division (73rd Brigade): the Bois en Hache, across the Souchez from the Pimple / suppressFrac**: 0.25 | estimate | estimate | no source measures it. The attack drew off fire that would have met the 10th Brigade: the Leinster 'were enfiladed by machine-gun fire from south of the Souchez stream' (BOH p. 348). A quarter of the Pimple garrison's fire is the builder's figure, for Plan 4 to calibrate (command.md) |
| **End conditions / lullMin**: 2880 | estimate | estimate | 48 hours. The longest pause between attacks in the battle was from the 10th Brigade's attack on Hill 145 (3 p.m. on 10 April, BOH p. 342) to the Pimple (5 a.m. on 12 April, BOH p. 345), about 38 hours, the result of the Northern Attack's being 'postponed for 48 hours' (BOH p. 343). A shorter lull would end a run that follows the historical course before the Pimple; 48 hours is the postponement itself (command.md) |
| **Local clock (minutes east of UTC)**: 60 | sourced | boh-1917-1, 147 n.1 (official) | 'Summer Time had come into use at midnight on the 24th' (of March 1917), so on 9 April the army's clock was GMT plus one hour; BOH gives German times converted, '2.30 a.m. (British time)' (p. 318 n.2). Check: Zero, 5:30, was 'set to coincide with first light' (Nicholson p. 253), and civil dawn at the frame origin on 9 April is 04:35 UTC, 05:35 on this clock |

The range runs from the brief's figure to BOH's located share (command.md) |

| **Attack bearing (frame)**: 75 | estimate | estimate | Measured off Nicholson 1962 Map 7, georeferenced with 17 village GCPs (rms 134 m): the dominant direction of the red 9 April attack arrows is 62° (4th Div), 74° (3rd), 87° (2nd) and 75° (1st); mean 74.6°, rounded to 75°. Expected error about ±5°; the divisions' arrows fan over 62-87°. Method in docs/research/vimy/sources.md. |
| **Geometry source (front and objective lines)**: Nicholson 1962 Map 7, georeferenced to IGN BD TOPO road and railway junctions (geometry.md) | sourced | nicholson-1962, Map 7 (facing p. 262) (official) | Lines and features digitized off the georeferenced map; every feature carries its own method and accuracy. |

## Doctrine parameters

| Value | How it is known | Source | Note |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **bef-1917 / common / baseMorale**: 0.8 — Morale a company starts the battle with, before leader and training adjustments (0 to 1). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). morale runs from 0 to 1. A company starts at about 0.8; with lossShock 1, losing a third of its men in a short time takes it below 0.5 and losing half takes it near 0.3. Both armies' infantry in April 1917 are taken as steady troops: 0.8, before leader and training adjustments; what the bombardment did to the German garrisons is the bombardment model's, not the doctrine's |
| **bef-1917 / common / goToGround**: 0.5 — Morale below which a company goes to ground. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). morale runs from 0 to 1. A company starts at about 0.8; with lossShock 1, losing a third of its men in a short time takes it below 0.5 and losing half takes it near 0.3. an attack usually went to ground when a company had lost about a third of its men in a few minutes: 0.5 |
| **bef-1917 / common / fallBack**: 0.35 — Morale below which a company falls back. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). morale runs from 0 to 1. A company starts at about 0.8; with lossShock 1, losing a third of its men in a short time takes it below 0.5 and losing half takes it near 0.3. a company that had lost about half its men in a short time could rarely be held: 0.35 |
| **bef-1917 / common / surrender**: 0.15 — Morale below which a company with an enemy close by surrenders. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). morale runs from 0 to 1. A company starts at about 0.8; with lossShock 1, losing a third of its men in a short time takes it below 0.5 and losing half takes it near 0.3. only a company close to breaking, with the enemy within bombing range, gives up: 0.15 |
| **bef-1917 / common / pinSuppression**: 0.7 — Suppression above which a company is pinned. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). suppression runs from 0 to 1; above 0.7 men under fire stay down whatever their morale |
| **bef-1917 / common / lossShock**: 1 — Morale lost per fraction of the company that becomes a casualty in a step. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). morale falls by the share of the company lost in the step: losing a tenth of its men costs a tenth of its morale. The simplest proportional rule, for Plan 4 to calibrate |
| **bef-1917 / common / suppressedDrain**: 0.3 — Morale lost per hour at full suppression. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). an hour pinned under full suppression wears a company's morale down by about a third of its starting value |
| **bef-1917 / common / isolationDrain**: 0.1 — Morale lost per hour while cut off from its headquarters. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a company cut off from its headquarters loses morale slowly: about a tenth an hour |
| **bef-1917 / common / recoveryPerHour**: 0.2 — Morale regained per hour when no fire is coming in. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). out of fire a company recovers, but more slowly than it was shaken: back from 0.4 to its start in about two hours |
| **bef-1917 / common / leaderMoraleStep**: 0.03 — Starting morale gained per point of the leader's morale rating above 3. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). leader ratings run from 1 to 5 around 3, so the best and worst leaders move starting morale by 0.06 either way, less than a tenth of its range |
| **bef-1917 / common / closeRangeM**: 50 — Distance in metres within which an enemy counts as close. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). bomb and bayonet range: a hand grenade is thrown some 30 m and a rush covers the rest |
| **bef-1917 / common / quietStepMin**: 10 — Minutes per simulation step while nothing is in contact. | estimate | estimate | a numerical choice, not behaviour: 10-minute steps while nothing is in contact keep a four-day run fast; contact and barrages switch to 2-minute steps |
| **bef-1917 / common / baseOrderDelayMin**: 15 — Minutes a headquarters takes to turn a report into an order. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a headquarters reads a report, decides and writes an order in some 10 to 30 minutes; 15, before the leader's orderSpeed rating and the channel's delay |
| **bef-1917 / common / marchKmPerHour**: 4 — Marching speed of reserves in kilometres per hour. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). about 2.5 miles an hour for formed infantry with halts on roads and tracks behind the front, slower than the 3 miles an hour of a road march in peace; no rate was found in the sources read |
| **bef-1917 / common / deployMin**: 30 — Minutes a reserve needs to deploy from column into line. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a battalion arriving in column needs about half an hour to get its companies into their places and its orders out before it can attack |
| **bef-1917 / common / reportEveryMin**: 30 — Minutes between routine reports to the next headquarters. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). battalions sent situation reports to brigade about every half hour in battle, and whenever something happened |
| **bef-1917 / common / fatiguePerKm**: 0.02 — Fatigue gained per kilometre marched or advanced. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a man carrying 60 lb or more through mud and shell holes tires quickly: 10 km of it brings fatigue to 0.2 on a scale of 0 to 1 |
| **bef-1917 / common / fatigueRecoveryPerHour**: 0.05 — Fatigue lost per hour of rest. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). resting in a trench or a shelter, men recover slowly: four hours take off 0.2 |
| **bef-1917 / ncoPenalty / rehearsed**: 0.1 — Share of speed and fire lost by a company with no officers that had rehearsed the attack. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). SS 143 trained every section to act under its own N.C.O.: "Section commanders control and lead their sections" and each man was trained to the Lewis gun or the rifle bomb (ss143-1917 pp. 12, 20); the Canadians rehearsed "repeatedly ... from platoon to divisional level" until "all ranks knew just where they would have to go and what to do" (nicholson-1962 p. 250). A rehearsed company that loses its officers goes on almost as well: a tenth |
| **bef-1917 / ncoPenalty / standard**: 0.3 — Share of speed and fire lost by a company with no officers and no special rehearsal. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a company that has not rehearsed its task and loses its officers hesitates and loses touch: about a third of its speed and fire, three times the rehearsed penalty |
| **bef-1917 / companyDefaults / officers**: 5 — Officers in a company when the scenario does not say. | estimate | estimate | one officer at the head of each of four platoons ("Headquarters - 1 officer and 4 O. R.", ss143-1917 p. 9) and the company commander; assumed, without a source read, that the second-in-command was left out of the battle |
| **bef-1917 / companyDefaults / machineGuns**: 0 — Machine guns in a company when the scenario does not say. | estimate | estimate | British Vickers guns were in the brigade machine-gun companies, not in the infantry companies; the scenario enters them as their own units or as mg-barrage artillery |
| **bef-1917 / companyDefaults / companiesPerBattalion**: 4 — Companies in a battalion when the scenario does not say. | estimate | estimate | the British battalion of four companies; every Canadian battalion in the scenario has four. No source read prints it (SS 143 gives the company of four platoons, ss143-1917 p. 22) |
| **bef-1917 / companyDefaults / dugoutDepthM**: 6.1 — Depth in metres of the dugouts a garrison company shelters in when the scenario does not say. | estimate | estimate | taken as the German figure (de-1917, twenty feet, from boh-1917-1 p. 316); no British figure was found in the sources read. Only a garrison company reads it, and no scenario so far has a British garrison under bombardment |
| **bef-1917 / companyDefaults / roundsPerMg**: 8000 — Rounds each machine gun has with it in the battle. | estimate | estimate | taken as the German figure (de-1917, 8,000 rounds a gun on the limbered gun wagons, german-handbook-1917 p. 52); no British figure for a gun's first-line load was found in the sources read. British companies carry no machine guns (machineGuns 0), so only a scenario that gives a company Vickers guns reads it |
| **bef-1917 / attack / followDistanceM**: 70 — How many metres behind the creeping barrage the attackers stay. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). kept from Plan 1 (the engine reads it). The leading wave kept as close behind the barrage as it dared: at Vimy the 2nd Brigade "rushed the sentries before the garrison could emerge" (boh-1917-1 p. 321), and at Passchendaele the infantry "followed their barrage so closely that in most cases the Germans could not man their machine-guns" (nicholson-1962 p. 324). SS 135, which would give the distance, was not found on the allowed hosts (doctrine.md, Open questions) |
| **bef-1917 / attack / maxAdvanceMPerMin**: 50 — Fastest walking pace of an advancing company in metres per minute. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). kept from Plan 1 (the engine reads it): about 3 km an hour over cratered ground, faster than the barrage's 100 yards in 3 minutes (30 m a minute, nicholson-1962 p. 253), so a company can keep up and close up after a check |
| **bef-1917 / attack / startJitterMin**: 2 — Each company starts up to this many minutes late, at random. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). kept from Plan 1 (the engine reads it): companies leave their jumping-off trenches within a minute or two of Zero |
| **bef-1917 / attack / speedJitterFrac**: 0.1 — Each company's speed varies at random by up to this fraction. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). kept from Plan 1 (the engine reads it): companies' paces differ by about a tenth with the ground |
| **bef-1917 / attack / waveSpacingM**: 70 — Distance in metres between successive waves of an attack. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). SS 143 shows the two waves of a platoon 50 to 100 yards apart (46 to 91 m) in the trench-to-trench attack (ss143-1917 Appendix I, p. 20; the figures are read from an OCR of the diagram, doctrine.md); 70 m, inside the range |
| **bef-1917 / attack / mopUpRateMenPerMin**: 3 — Men a mopping-up company clears out of dugouts per minute. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). moppers-up "work in pairs", "guard the entrances to dugouts" and do not go down "before the platoon for which they are mopping up arrives" (ss143-1917 p. 16): a dugout of 20-40 men, bombed and called on to surrender, comes up in some minutes; a company's pairs at several entrances clear about 3 men a minute |
| **bef-1917 / attack / stallToleranceMin**: 30 — Minutes an attack can stand still before it counts as stalled. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a company that has stood still for half an hour has lost its barrage by 900 m and counts as stalled |
| **bef-1917 / attack / minAttackRatio**: 1.5 — Attackers to defenders required before a commander orders a new attack. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a commander orders a fresh attack with at least half as many men again as the defenders in front; the capture itself needs captureRatio (3 to 1) locally, which the barrage and surprise give |
| **bef-1917 / attack / trainingBonusRehearsed**: 0.1 — Starting morale added for a company that rehearsed the attack. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). troops who had rehearsed on a full-scale replica until "all ranks knew just where they would have to go and what to do when they got there" (nicholson-1962 p. 250) start steadier: a tenth of the morale range |
| **bef-1917 / attack / ammoReserveFrac**: 0.2 — Share of ammunition a company keeps back and will not fire away. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). every man carried at least 120 rounds (ss143-1917 p. 10), with carrying parties for more; a fifth is kept back against a counter-attack |
| **bef-1917 / defence / emergeMin**: 3 — Minutes after a barrage lifts before the garrison is out of its dugouts and firing. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the same climb out of a dugout as the Germans': 3 minutes (de-1917) |
| **bef-1917 / defence / trappedSurrenderFrac**: 0.5 — Share of a garrison that surrenders when attackers reach it before it emerges. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). British dugouts were fewer and shallower; half of a trapped garrison gives up |
| **bef-1917 / defence / localCounterAttackDelayMin**: 10 — Minutes, once the reserve's headquarters knows a segment is lost, for the local reserve to form up and go in; its approach is simulated movement after this delay. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the form-up and launch only, counted from when the battalion's picture shows the loss (the approach is simulated movement): British troops holding captured ground counter-attacked locally to restore a lost post, with less doctrine for the immediate counter-attack than the Germans (de-1917 has 5): 10 |
| **bef-1917 / defence / divisionalCounterAttackDelayMin**: 60 — Minutes, once the reserves have arrived and deployed, before the divisional counter-attack goes in; their march and deployment are counted separately (marchKmPerHour, deployMin). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the form-up and launch only, counted from when the reserves have arrived and deployed (their march and deployment are counted separately): a British counter-attack waited for orders down two levels and a barrage to be arranged, about an hour (de-1917 has 30) |
| **bef-1917 / defence / counterAttackMinRatio**: 1 — Defenders to attackers needed before a counter-attack is ordered. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). British commanders counter-attacked with at least equal numbers |
| **bef-1917 / defence / commitReserveWhenLostFrac**: 0.5 — Share of a sector's segments that must be lost before its reserve is committed. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a British reserve holding a captured line goes in when half the line is lost |
| **de-1917 / common / baseMorale**: 0.8 — Morale a company starts the battle with, before leader and training adjustments (0 to 1). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). morale runs from 0 to 1. A company starts at about 0.8; with lossShock 1, losing a third of its men in a short time takes it below 0.5 and losing half takes it near 0.3. Both armies' infantry in April 1917 are taken as steady troops: 0.8, before leader and training adjustments; what the bombardment did to the German garrisons is the bombardment model's, not the doctrine's |
| **de-1917 / common / goToGround**: 0.5 — Morale below which a company goes to ground. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). morale runs from 0 to 1. A company starts at about 0.8; with lossShock 1, losing a third of its men in a short time takes it below 0.5 and losing half takes it near 0.3. an attack usually went to ground when a company had lost about a third of its men in a few minutes: 0.5 |
| **de-1917 / common / fallBack**: 0.35 — Morale below which a company falls back. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). morale runs from 0 to 1. A company starts at about 0.8; with lossShock 1, losing a third of its men in a short time takes it below 0.5 and losing half takes it near 0.3. a company that had lost about half its men in a short time could rarely be held: 0.35 |
| **de-1917 / common / surrender**: 0.15 — Morale below which a company with an enemy close by surrenders. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). morale runs from 0 to 1. A company starts at about 0.8; with lossShock 1, losing a third of its men in a short time takes it below 0.5 and losing half takes it near 0.3. only a company close to breaking, with the enemy within bombing range, gives up: 0.15 |
| **de-1917 / common / pinSuppression**: 0.7 — Suppression above which a company is pinned. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). suppression runs from 0 to 1; above 0.7 men under fire stay down whatever their morale |
| **de-1917 / common / lossShock**: 1 — Morale lost per fraction of the company that becomes a casualty in a step. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). morale falls by the share of the company lost in the step: losing a tenth of its men costs a tenth of its morale. The simplest proportional rule, for Plan 4 to calibrate |
| **de-1917 / common / suppressedDrain**: 0.3 — Morale lost per hour at full suppression. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). an hour pinned under full suppression wears a company's morale down by about a third of its starting value |
| **de-1917 / common / isolationDrain**: 0.1 — Morale lost per hour while cut off from its headquarters. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a company cut off from its headquarters loses morale slowly: about a tenth an hour |
| **de-1917 / common / recoveryPerHour**: 0.2 — Morale regained per hour when no fire is coming in. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). out of fire a company recovers, but more slowly than it was shaken: back from 0.4 to its start in about two hours |
| **de-1917 / common / leaderMoraleStep**: 0.03 — Starting morale gained per point of the leader's morale rating above 3. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). leader ratings run from 1 to 5 around 3, so the best and worst leaders move starting morale by 0.06 either way, less than a tenth of its range |
| **de-1917 / common / closeRangeM**: 50 — Distance in metres within which an enemy counts as close. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). bomb and bayonet range: a hand grenade is thrown some 30 m and a rush covers the rest |
| **de-1917 / common / quietStepMin**: 10 — Minutes per simulation step while nothing is in contact. | estimate | estimate | a numerical choice, not behaviour: 10-minute steps while nothing is in contact keep a four-day run fast; contact and barrages switch to 2-minute steps |
| **de-1917 / common / baseOrderDelayMin**: 15 — Minutes a headquarters takes to turn a report into an order. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a headquarters reads a report, decides and writes an order in some 10 to 30 minutes; 15, before the leader's orderSpeed rating and the channel's delay |
| **de-1917 / common / marchKmPerHour**: 4 — Marching speed of reserves in kilometres per hour. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). about 2.5 miles an hour for formed infantry with halts on roads and tracks behind the front, slower than the 3 miles an hour of a road march in peace; no rate was found in the sources read |
| **de-1917 / common / deployMin**: 30 — Minutes a reserve needs to deploy from column into line. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a battalion arriving in column needs about half an hour to get its companies into their places and its orders out before it can attack |
| **de-1917 / common / reportEveryMin**: 30 — Minutes between routine reports to the next headquarters. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). battalions sent situation reports to brigade about every half hour in battle, and whenever something happened |
| **de-1917 / common / fatiguePerKm**: 0.02 — Fatigue gained per kilometre marched or advanced. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a man carrying 60 lb or more through mud and shell holes tires quickly: 10 km of it brings fatigue to 0.2 on a scale of 0 to 1 |
| **de-1917 / common / fatigueRecoveryPerHour**: 0.05 — Fatigue lost per hour of rest. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). resting in a trench or a shelter, men recover slowly: four hours take off 0.2 |
| **de-1917 / ncoPenalty / rehearsed**: 0.1 — Share of speed and fire lost by a company with no officers that had rehearsed the attack. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). German doctrine of December 1916 made the group of about eleven men under an N.C.O. "the infantry's tactical unit", its leader free "within certain limits, to retire in any direction before strong enemy fire" (nicholson-1962 p. 240); a German company that had rehearsed a set task (a counter-attack, say) loses as little as a rehearsed British one: a tenth |
| **de-1917 / ncoPenalty / standard**: 0.3 — Share of speed and fire lost by a company with no officers and no special rehearsal. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a company that has not rehearsed its task and loses its officers hesitates and loses touch: about a third of its speed and fire, three times the rehearsed penalty |
| **de-1917 / companyDefaults / officers**: 3 — Officers in a company when the scenario does not say. | estimate | estimate | the war establishment of a company was 5 officers (german-handbook-1917 p. 39), but "a company [is now usually commanded] by a subaltern" (p. 39) and companies in the line had fewer: 3 |
| **de-1917 / companyDefaults / lewisGuns**: 1 — Light machine guns (the side's, fired as companyWeapons.lightMg: here the 08/15) in a company when the scenario does not say. | estimate | estimate | the German light machine gun, the 08/15, fired as companyWeapons.lightMg (mg08-15); the count keeps the scenario's field name. "The issue of the '08/'15 light machine gun began in March, 1917, on the scale of 3 to every infantry company" (german-handbook-1918 p. 61; the order of 12 March 1917, p. 44), and "By the end of 1917 every infantry company on the Western Front had received 3" (p. 55). In April 1917 the issue had only begun: 1 |
| **de-1917 / companyDefaults / machineGuns**: 1.5 — Machine guns in a company when the scenario does not say. | estimate | estimate | every infantry regiment has three machine-gun companies, one attached to each battalion (german-handbook-1917 p. 49), of 6 guns (p. 50); the scenario has no machine-gun companies, so the battalion's 6 guns are spread over its four rifle companies: 1.5 |
| **de-1917 / companyDefaults / bombsPerMan**: 2 — Grenades each man carries when the scenario does not say. | estimate | estimate | taken as the British figure (2, ss143-1917 p. 10): no German figure was found in the sources read, and both armies fought for trenches with the bomb |
| **de-1917 / companyDefaults / strength**: 187 — Men in a company when the scenario does not say. | estimate | estimate | the Prussian War Ministry order of 12 March 1917 set a battalion at 750 other ranks (german-handbook-1918 p. 44), a quarter each to four companies; the earlier war establishment was 259 a company (german-handbook-1917 p. 39) |
| **de-1917 / companyDefaults / dugoutDepthM**: 6.1 — Depth in metres of the dugouts a garrison company shelters in when the scenario does not say. | estimate | estimate | twenty feet, the shallow end of the deep mined dug-outs: on Vimy Ridge "the main German dug-outs and tunnels twenty to fifty feet down in the chalk" (boh-1917-1 p. 316). A garrison the scenario gives no depth for is taken to have the shallowest of them; Vimy's garrisons each have their own depth |
| **de-1917 / companyDefaults / roundsPerLightMg**: 4000 — Rounds each light machine gun (the side's, fired as companyWeapons.lightMg) has with it in the battle. | estimate | estimate | half the heavy gun's 8,000 rounds on its limber (german-handbook-1917 p. 52): the 08/15 had no limber and its team carried its belt boxes by hand, so less, but a trench garrison kept boxes at the gun. Order of magnitude, for Plan 4 to check |
| **de-1917 / attack / followDistanceM**: 0 — How many metres behind the creeping barrage the attackers stay. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). kept from the Plan 1 stub: German counter-attacks are not modelled as following a creeping barrage; the engine reads this value, so it is unchanged (the golden record would move) |
| **de-1917 / attack / maxAdvanceMPerMin**: 40 — Fastest walking pace of an advancing company in metres per minute. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). kept from the Plan 1 stub (the engine reads it). A German order of 1918 gives "Two hundred meters in four minutes, synchronized with the rolling barrage" (survey-german-tactics-1918 p. 18), 50 m a minute; a counter-attack over broken ground goes slower |
| **de-1917 / attack / startJitterMin**: 0 — Each company starts up to this many minutes late, at random. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). kept from the Plan 1 stub (the engine reads it) |
| **de-1917 / attack / speedJitterFrac**: 0 — Each company's speed varies at random by up to this fraction. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). kept from the Plan 1 stub (the engine reads it) |
| **de-1917 / attack / waveSpacingM**: 50 — Distance in metres between successive waves of an attack. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). German counter-attack troops "advance in waves" (survey-german-tactics-1918 p. 35); no distance is given, so the British figure's lower end: 50 |
| **de-1917 / attack / mopUpRateMenPerMin**: 3 — Men a mopping-up company clears out of dugouts per minute. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). taken as the British figure: no German source on clearing dugouts was found, and German troops retaking a trench face the same stairways as British moppers-up, who "work in pairs" and "guard the entrances to dugouts" (ss143-1917 p. 16): about 3 men a minute |
| **de-1917 / attack / stallToleranceMin**: 30 — Minutes an attack can stand still before it counts as stalled. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a company that has stood still for half an hour has lost its barrage by 900 m and counts as stalled |
| **de-1917 / attack / minAttackRatio**: 1.5 — Attackers to defenders required before a commander orders a new attack. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a commander orders a fresh attack with at least half as many men again as the defenders in front; the capture itself needs captureRatio (3 to 1) locally, which the barrage and surprise give |
| **de-1917 / attack / trainingBonusRehearsed**: 0.1 — Starting morale added for a company that rehearsed the attack. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). taken as the British figure: no German source on the effect of rehearsal was found, and a set task practised beforehand steadies any troops as it did the Canadians (nicholson-1962 p. 250): a tenth of the morale range |
| **de-1917 / attack / ammoReserveFrac**: 0.2 — Share of ammunition a company keeps back and will not fire away. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). taken as the British figure: no German figure was found, and a counter-attacking company has the same need to keep rounds back against the next attack: a fifth |
| **de-1917 / defence / emergeMin**: 3 — Minutes after a barrage lifts before the garrison is out of its dugouts and firing. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the main German dug-outs on Vimy Ridge were "twenty to fifty feet down in the chalk" (boh-1917-1 p. 316): a garrison has to climb 6-15 m of stairs with rifles and machine guns once its sentries call; 1 to 5 minutes, 3. BOH: "The attacking infantry could not hope to reach and block the entrances before the garrisons turned out unless tactical surprise were obtained" (p. 316) |
| **de-1917 / defence / trappedSurrenderFrac**: 0.6 — Share of a garrison that surrenders when attackers reach it before it emerges. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a garrison caught below ground with bombers at the stairheads mostly gave up: on the 3rd Canadian Division's front "a like number of the enemy were captured shortly afterwards in the dug-outs" (boh-1917-1 p. 325); some fought on or hid. 0.6, the rest left as dugout points to be cleared |
| **de-1917 / defence / localCounterAttackDelayMin**: 5 — Minutes, once the reserve's headquarters knows a segment is lost, for the local reserve to form up and go in; its approach is simulated movement after this delay. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the form-up and launch only, counted from when the battalion's picture shows the loss: the report's journey is the channels' and the approach to the lost trench is simulated movement after this delay. German doctrine called for "immediate counter-attacks ... to recover any ground lost" (boh-1917-1 p. 316), the Gegenstoss "frequently carried out in the course of the battle" as opposed to the deliberate Gegenangriff (p. 523 n.), before the enemy can "rest or install himself" (survey-german-tactics-1918 p. 35), by a counter-attack company that lies close behind with its task known (p. 32). A few minutes to come up from its shelters, form up and go: 5 |
| **de-1917 / defence / divisionalCounterAttackDelayMin**: 30 — Minutes, once the reserves have arrived and deployed, before the divisional counter-attack goes in; their march and deployment are counted separately (marchKmPerHour, deployMin). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the form-up and launch only, counted from when the reserves have arrived and deployed: their order's journey, their march and their deployment are counted separately. The counter-attack (Eingreif) divisions were to strike "the enemy in the midst of his assault" (nicholson-1962 p. 306) from "close behind the Second Line" (p. 240), so they go in from the march: orders to the battalions and the artillery's support arranged in about half an hour. The deliberate Gegenangriff, prepared over days (boh-1917-1 p. 523 n.), is not this. Where a battle's reserves actually stood is the scenario's, not the doctrine's |
| **de-1917 / defence / counterAttackMinRatio**: 0.75 — Defenders to attackers needed before a counter-attack is ordered. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). German counter-attacks relied on fresh troops striking tired ones: "their fresh troops hold a great advantage over the tired troops of the attacker" (survey-german-tactics-1918 p. 35); so they went in without numerical superiority, at three to four |
| **de-1917 / defence / commitReserveWhenLostFrac**: 0.25 — Share of a sector's segments that must be lost before its reserve is committed. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the elastic defence expected to lose ground in the forward zone and won it back by counter-attack (nicholson-1962 pp. 239-240); the reserve goes in once a quarter of a sector is lost |
| **bef-1917 / companyDefaults / lewisGuns**: 4 — Lewis guns (the side's light machine guns, fired as companyWeapons.lightMg) in a company when the scenario does not say. | sourced | ss143-1917, 9, 22 (primary) | one Lewis gun section in each platoon (p. 9) and four platoons in a company (Appendix III, p. 22) |
| **bef-1917 / companyDefaults / bombsPerMan**: 2 — Grenades each man carries when the scenario does not say. | sourced | ss143-1917, 10 (primary) | in the trench-to-trench attack "every man ... carries at least 120 rounds S.A.A. and 2 or more bombs"; bombers carry more |
| **bef-1917 / companyDefaults / strength**: 158 — Men in a company when the scenario does not say. | sourced | ss143-1917, 22 (primary) | "The Company, Taking 4 Average Strength Platoons of 36 O.R. and Coy. H.Q. 14" (Appendix III): 4 x 36 + 14; an example average strength, not an establishment |
| **bef-1917 / companyDefaults / roundsPerRifle**: 120 — Rounds of small-arms ammunition each rifleman carries into the battle (the company's load, with its light and heavy machine guns' rounds, is what its fire uses up). | sourced | ss143-1917, 10 (primary) | in the trench-to-trench attack "every man ... carries at least 120 rounds S.A.A."; bombers, signallers, scouts, runners and Lewis gunners carry 50, and the company's load is counted by weapon, so the 50 of the Lewis gunners are not added |
| **bef-1917 / companyDefaults / roundsPerLightMg**: 1410 — Rounds each light machine gun (the side's, fired as companyWeapons.lightMg) has with it in the battle. | sourced | ss143-1917, 10 (primary) | "The Lewis gun section carries 30 drums", each of 47 rounds ("The magazine, which holds forty-seven rounds", lewis-1917 p. 18): 30 x 47 |
| **de-1917 / companyDefaults / companiesPerBattalion**: 4 — Companies in a battalion when the scenario does not say. | sourced | german-handbook-1917, 30 (primary) | "Each battalion is divided into 4 companies and a machine gun company" |
| **de-1917 / companyDefaults / roundsPerRifle**: 150 — Rounds of small-arms ammunition each rifleman carries into the battle (the company's load, with its light and heavy machine guns' rounds, is what its fire uses up). | sourced | german-handbook-1917, 44 (primary) | the distribution of ammunition in the field, rounds per man: "Carried on the soldier ... 150"; another 70 a man are in the company ammunition wagon, behind the line |
| **de-1917 / companyDefaults / roundsPerMg**: 8000 — Rounds each machine gun has with it in the battle. | sourced | german-handbook-1917, 52 (primary) | "The amount now carried appears to be - 8,000 rounds per gun on the limbered gun wagons", with 1,300 more on the ammunition wagons; the limbered wagons' rounds come forward with the gun |

## Model tables

| Value | How it is known | Source | Note |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **weapons / rifle / rpm**: 5 — Rounds one weapon fires per minute over a sustained engagement (a barrage, a bombardment or a firefight of some minutes), not its burst rate. | estimate | estimate | aimed fire kept up in action; the rapid rates are for a minute or so: 20-30 aimed shots a minute for the SMLE (wiki-lee-enfield) and 15 for the Gewehr 98 (wiki-gewehr-98), whose "maximum rate of fire obtainable" is 35 to 40 (german-handbook-1917, the rifle section). Men moving, loading under fire or looking for a target fire far less; a third of the slower rapid rate, 5 |
| **weapons / rifle / rangeScaleM**: 400 — Distance in metres over which the weapon's effect falls by a factor of e (about 2.7), for the spread of its fire with range. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). aimed rifle fire is effective to about 500 m (the SMLE's 550-yard effective range, wiki-lee-enfield; the Gewehr 98's 500 m with iron sights, wiki-gewehr-98): 400 m leaves a quarter of the point-blank effect at 550 m and almost none at 2 km |
| **weapons / rifle / lethality**: 0.03 — Men hit per round, on average, when the weapon fires on a target group of full density standing in the open at close range; cover, range, sight and suppression reduce it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). one hit in about 30 aimed rounds at a dense group in the open at close range; with rangeScaleM a rifleman then hits 0.07 men a minute at 300 m, so a machine gun (3.6 a minute at 300 m) is worth about 50 rifles, the builder's order of magnitude, not a figure from a source read (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / rifle / suppressPerRound**: 0.05 — Suppression one round adds to its target, before dividing by the target's men: 100 rounds at 0.1 on a 100-man company add 0.1 (1 is fully suppressed). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). half a machine-gun bullet's: aimed rounds come singly; a 100-rifle company firing 5 rounds a minute each adds 0.25 a minute to a 100-man target |
| **weapons / lewis / rangeScaleM**: 500 — Distance in metres over which the weapon's effect falls by a factor of e (about 2.7), for the spread of its fire with range. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a bipod gun, lighter and less steady than a tripod machine gun: its effective range is 880 yards (805 m, wiki-lewis-gun), where 500 m leaves a fifth of the point-blank effect |
| **weapons / lewis / lethality**: 0.01 — Men hit per round, on average, when the weapon fires on a target group of full density standing in the open at close range; cover, range, sight and suppression reduce it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). bursts spread more than aimed rifle rounds: one hit in 100 rounds at a dense group close by; at 300 m and 250 rounds a minute that is 1.4 men a minute, a little under half a tripod machine gun |
| **weapons / lewis / suppressPerRound**: 0.08 — Suppression one round adds to its target, before dividing by the target's men: 100 rounds at 0.1 on a 100-man company add 0.1 (1 is fully suppressed). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). automatic fire suppresses more per round than aimed rifle fire, less than a tripod gun's beaten zone; between the rifle's 0.05 and the machine gun's 0.1 |
| **weapons / vickers / rangeScaleM**: 1000 — Distance in metres over which the weapon's effect falls by a factor of e (about 2.7), for the spread of its fire with range. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a tripod machine gun's fire stays dense far out (the MG 08 is sighted to 2,200 yards, german-handbook-1917 p. 51): 1,000 m leaves three-quarters of the point-blank effect at 300 m and a tenth at 2,300 m, where indirect barrage fire works |
| **weapons / vickers / lethality**: 0.012 — Men hit per round, on average, when the weapon fires on a target group of full density standing in the open at close range; cover, range, sight and suppression reduce it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the plan's check: a gun firing about 400 rounds a minute at an exposed company moving in the open at 300 m should cause casualties in single figures a minute; 400 x 0.012 x exp(-300/1000) = 3.6 a minute |
| **weapons / vickers / suppressPerRound**: 0.1 — Suppression one round adds to its target, before dividing by the target's men: 100 rounds at 0.1 on a 100-man company add 0.1 (1 is fully suppressed). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a gun's 400 rounds a minute on a 100-man company add 0.4 a minute, so sustained fire pins it (0.7) inside two minutes, less the decay (suppressionHalfLifeMin) |
| **weapons / mg08 / rangeScaleM**: 1000 — Distance in metres over which the weapon's effect falls by a factor of e (about 2.7), for the spread of its fire with range. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a tripod machine gun's fire stays dense far out (the MG 08 is sighted to 2,200 yards, german-handbook-1917 p. 51): 1,000 m leaves three-quarters of the point-blank effect at 300 m and a tenth at 2,300 m, where indirect barrage fire works |
| **weapons / mg08 / lethality**: 0.012 — Men hit per round, on average, when the weapon fires on a target group of full density standing in the open at close range; cover, range, sight and suppression reduce it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the plan's check: a gun firing about 400 rounds a minute at an exposed company moving in the open at 300 m should cause casualties in single figures a minute; 400 x 0.012 x exp(-300/1000) = 3.6 a minute |
| **weapons / mg08 / suppressPerRound**: 0.1 — Suppression one round adds to its target, before dividing by the target's men: 100 rounds at 0.1 on a 100-man company add 0.1 (1 is fully suppressed). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a gun's 400 rounds a minute on a 100-man company add 0.4 a minute, so sustained fire pins it (0.7) inside two minutes, less the decay (suppressionHalfLifeMin) |
| **weapons / mg08-15 / rpm**: 250 — Rounds one weapon fires per minute over a sustained engagement (a barrage, a bombardment or a firefight of some minutes), not its burst rate. | estimate | estimate | no rate for the 08/15 was found in the sources read. It is the MG 08 made light, fed from belts of "either 100 or 250 rounds" (german-handbook-1918 p. 61), fired from a bipod by one gunner; in action, bursts and target changes. Taken as the practical rate of the British light gun, the Lewis (250 in traversing fire, lewis-1917 p. 81), the same class of weapon: 250 |
| **weapons / mg08-15 / rangeScaleM**: 600 — Distance in metres over which the weapon's effect falls by a factor of e (about 2.7), for the spread of its fire with range. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the MG 08's ballistics (german-handbook-1918 p. 61) from a bipod, "not so accurate": between the Lewis gun's 500 m and the tripod MG 08's 1,000 m |
| **weapons / mg08-15 / lethality**: 0.01 — Men hit per round, on average, when the weapon fires on a target group of full density standing in the open at close range; cover, range, sight and suppression reduce it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). "not so accurate" as the MG 08 (german-handbook-1918 p. 61): a little under its 0.012, as the Lewis gun's 0.01; at 300 m and 250 rounds a minute, 250 x 0.01 x exp(-300/600) = 1.5 men a minute |
| **weapons / mg08-15 / suppressPerRound**: 0.08 — Suppression one round adds to its target, before dividing by the target's men: 100 rounds at 0.1 on a 100-man company add 0.1 (1 is fully suppressed). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). automatic fire from a light gun, as the Lewis gun: between the rifle's 0.05 and the machine gun's 0.1 |
| **weapons / 18pdr / rangeScaleM**: 11900 — Distance in metres over which the weapon's effect falls by a factor of e (about 2.7), for the spread of its fire with range. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 2 x maxRangeM: an observed or registered gun keeps most of its accuracy across its range, losing about 40 per cent at its longest range (exp(-0.5)); order of magnitude only (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / 18pdr / lethality**: 0.5 — Men hit per round, on average, when the weapon fires on a target group of full density standing in the open at close range; cover, range, sight and suppression reduce it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 0.5 x sqrt(shellKg / 8.4): the 18-pdr's 0.5 men hit per round on a dense group in the open (one shrapnel or HE burst among a platoon in the open hitting about one man in two rounds), scaled by the square root of shell weight because a burst's lethal area grows more slowly than its weight; order of magnitude only, for Plan 4 to calibrate (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / 18pdr / suppressPerRound**: 3 — Suppression one round adds to its target, before dividing by the target's men: 100 rounds at 0.1 on a 100-man company add 0.1 (1 is fully suppressed). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 6 x lethality: one burst near a 100-man company adds about 0.03 suppression for the 18-pdr, so its 9 rounds a minute on 100 m of front pin a company inside two or three minutes; the same ratio for every shell (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / 13pdr / rpm**: 2 — Rounds one weapon fires per minute over a sustained engagement (a barrage, a bombardment or a firefight of some minutes), not its burst rate. | estimate | estimate | sustained rate: the 18-pdr, limited to 4 rounds a minute from January 1917 (wiki-qf-18pdr, after Farndale), fired the Vimy barrage at 2 after its first three minutes (nicholson-1962 p. 253), half its limit; the 13-pdr, a lighter quick-firing gun of the same family, is given the same 2 |
| **weapons / 13pdr / rangeScaleM**: 10800 — Distance in metres over which the weapon's effect falls by a factor of e (about 2.7), for the spread of its fire with range. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 2 x maxRangeM: an observed or registered gun keeps most of its accuracy across its range, losing about 40 per cent at its longest range (exp(-0.5)); order of magnitude only (tables.md, Coefficients) |

| **weapons / 13pdr / suppressPerRound**: 2.5 — Suppression one round adds to its target, before dividing by the target's men: 100 rounds at 0.1 on a 100-man company add 0.1 (1 is fully suppressed). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 6 x lethality: one burst near a 100-man company adds about 0.03 suppression for the 18-pdr, so its 9 rounds a minute on 100 m of front pin a company inside two or three minutes; the same ratio for every shell (tables.md, Coefficients) |
