| **weapons / 105mm-lfh / lethality**: 0.68 — Men hit per round, on average, when the weapon fires on a target group of full density standing in the open at close range; cover, range, sight and suppression reduce it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 0.5 x sqrt(shellKg / 8.4): the 18-pdr's 0.5 men hit per round on a dense group in the open (one shrapnel or HE burst among a platoon in the open hitting about one man in two rounds), scaled by the square root of shell weight because a burst's lethal area grows more slowly than its weight; order of magnitude only, for Plan 4 to calibrate (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / 105mm-lfh / suppressPerRound**: 4.1 — Suppression one round adds to its target, before dividing by the target's men: 100 rounds at 0.1 on a 100-man company add 0.1 (1 is fully suppressed). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 6 x lethality: one burst near a 100-man company adds about 0.03 suppression for the 18-pdr, so its 9 rounds a minute on 100 m of front pin a company inside two or three minutes; the same ratio for every shell (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / 150mm-sfh / rpm**: 1 — Rounds one weapon fires per minute over a sustained engagement (a barrage, a bombardment or a firefight of some minutes), not its burst rate. | estimate | estimate | wiki-sfh-13 gives 3 a minute; a third, as for the other heavy pieces: 1 |
| **weapons / 150mm-sfh / rangeScaleM**: 17000 — Distance in metres over which the weapon's effect falls by a factor of e (about 2.7), for the spread of its fire with range. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 2 x maxRangeM: an observed or registered gun keeps most of its accuracy across its range, losing about 40 per cent at its longest range (exp(-0.5)); order of magnitude only (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / 150mm-sfh / lethality**: 1.1 — Men hit per round, on average, when the weapon fires on a target group of full density standing in the open at close range; cover, range, sight and suppression reduce it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 0.5 x sqrt(shellKg / 8.4): the 18-pdr's 0.5 men hit per round on a dense group in the open (one shrapnel or HE burst among a platoon in the open hitting about one man in two rounds), scaled by the square root of shell weight because a burst's lethal area grows more slowly than its weight; order of magnitude only, for Plan 4 to calibrate (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / 150mm-sfh / suppressPerRound**: 6.6 — Suppression one round adds to its target, before dividing by the target's men: 100 rounds at 0.1 on a 100-man company add 0.1 (1 is fully suppressed). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 6 x lethality: one burst near a 100-man company adds about 0.03 suppression for the 18-pdr, so its 9 rounds a minute on 100 m of front pin a company inside two or three minutes; the same ratio for every shell (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / 210mm-mrs / rpm**: 0.5 — Rounds one weapon fires per minute over a sustained engagement (a barrage, a bombardment or a firefight of some minutes), not its burst rate. | estimate | estimate | a 184 lb shell, as the 8-inch howitzer: one round in two minutes (no rate found) |
| **weapons / 210mm-mrs / rangeScaleM**: 18200 — Distance in metres over which the weapon's effect falls by a factor of e (about 2.7), for the spread of its fire with range. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 2 x maxRangeM: an observed or registered gun keeps most of its accuracy across its range, losing about 40 per cent at its longest range (exp(-0.5)); order of magnitude only (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / 210mm-mrs / lethality**: 1.6 — Men hit per round, on average, when the weapon fires on a target group of full density standing in the open at close range; cover, range, sight and suppression reduce it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 0.5 x sqrt(shellKg / 8.4): the 18-pdr's 0.5 men hit per round on a dense group in the open (one shrapnel or HE burst among a platoon in the open hitting about one man in two rounds), scaled by the square root of shell weight because a burst's lethal area grows more slowly than its weight; order of magnitude only, for Plan 4 to calibrate (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / 210mm-mrs / suppressPerRound**: 9.6 — Suppression one round adds to its target, before dividing by the target's men: 100 rounds at 0.1 on a 100-man company add 0.1 (1 is fully suppressed). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 6 x lethality: one burst near a 100-man company adds about 0.03 suppression for the 18-pdr, so its 9 rounds a minute on 100 m of front pin a company inside two or three minutes; the same ratio for every shell (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / minenwerfer-light / rpm**: 4 — Rounds one weapon fires per minute over a sustained engagement (a barrage, a bombardment or a firefight of some minutes), not its burst rate. | estimate | estimate | the light Minenwerfer fires "up to 20 rounds a minute for short periods" (german-handbook-1918 p. 104); sustained, a fifth: 4, a little under the Stokes's 6 |
| **weapons / minenwerfer-light / rangeScaleM**: 2100 — Distance in metres over which the weapon's effect falls by a factor of e (about 2.7), for the spread of its fire with range. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 2 x maxRangeM: an observed or registered gun keeps most of its accuracy across its range, losing about 40 per cent at its longest range (exp(-0.5)); order of magnitude only (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / minenwerfer-light / lethality**: 0.37 — Men hit per round, on average, when the weapon fires on a target group of full density standing in the open at close range; cover, range, sight and suppression reduce it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 0.5 x sqrt(shellKg / 8.4): the 18-pdr's 0.5 men hit per round on a dense group in the open (one shrapnel or HE burst among a platoon in the open hitting about one man in two rounds), scaled by the square root of shell weight because a burst's lethal area grows more slowly than its weight; order of magnitude only, for Plan 4 to calibrate (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / minenwerfer-light / suppressPerRound**: 2.2 — Suppression one round adds to its target, before dividing by the target's men: 100 rounds at 0.1 on a 100-man company add 0.1 (1 is fully suppressed). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 6 x lethality: one burst near a 100-man company adds about 0.03 suppression for the 18-pdr, so its 9 rounds a minute on 100 m of front pin a company inside two or three minutes; the same ratio for every shell (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / minenwerfer-medium / rangeScaleM**: 1800 — Distance in metres over which the weapon's effect falls by a factor of e (about 2.7), for the spread of its fire with range. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 2 x maxRangeM: an observed or registered gun keeps most of its accuracy across its range, losing about 40 per cent at its longest range (exp(-0.5)); order of magnitude only (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / minenwerfer-medium / lethality**: 1.2 — Men hit per round, on average, when the weapon fires on a target group of full density standing in the open at close range; cover, range, sight and suppression reduce it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 0.5 x sqrt(shellKg / 8.4): the 18-pdr's 0.5 men hit per round on a dense group in the open (one shrapnel or HE burst among a platoon in the open hitting about one man in two rounds), scaled by the square root of shell weight because a burst's lethal area grows more slowly than its weight; order of magnitude only, for Plan 4 to calibrate (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **weapons / minenwerfer-medium / suppressPerRound**: 7.2 — Suppression one round adds to its target, before dividing by the target's men: 100 rounds at 0.1 on a 100-man company add 0.1 (1 is fully suppressed). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). 6 x lethality: one burst near a 100-man company adds about 0.03 suppression for the 18-pdr, so its 9 rounds a minute on 100 m of front pin a company inside two or three minutes; the same ratio for every shell (tables.md, Coefficients) |
| **cover / moving / open**: 1 — Share of fire that still hurts a man walking or running upright on open ground (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the reference: an upright man in the open gets no protection (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / moving / cratered**: 0.8 — Share of fire that still hurts a man walking or running upright on shell-cratered ground (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). moving through craters, men drop into shell holes at each halt and the lips screen them from grazing fire part of the time (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / moving / wood**: 0.6 — Share of fire that still hurts a man walking or running upright in a wood (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). trees and undergrowth hide men from aimed fire and stop some bullets, though shells burst in the branches; a little under half the fire is lost (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / moving / village**: 0.6 — Share of fire that still hurts a man walking or running upright in a village (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). walls and rubble screen men moving between houses as a wood does (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / moving / trench**: 0.5 — Share of fire that still hurts a man walking or running upright on trench ground (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). moving along or across a trench system men are in the trench, or a step from it, about half the time (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / prone / open**: 0.4 — Share of fire that still hurts a man lying down on open ground (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a prone man presents about a third of an upright man's target to bullets; shrapnel from above still reaches him (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / prone / cratered**: 0.25 — Share of fire that still hurts a man lying down on shell-cratered ground (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). lying in a shell hole: protected from bullets except from close above, and from all but nearby bursts (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / prone / wood**: 0.3 — Share of fire that still hurts a man lying down in a wood (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). prone among trees and roots: between open and cratered ground (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / prone / village**: 0.25 — Share of fire that still hurts a man lying down in a village (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). prone behind rubble and walls: about as good as a shell hole (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / prone / trench**: 0.2 — Share of fire that still hurts a man lying down on trench ground (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). prone on trench ground is mostly prone in a broken trench or its parados: close to in-trench (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / in-trench / open**: 0.2 — Share of fire that still hurts a man in a trench, firing or keeping watch on open ground (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a man in a fire trench shows head and shoulders while firing and nothing when not; trench cover, the ground around it matters little (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / in-trench / cratered**: 0.2 — Share of fire that still hurts a man in a trench, firing or keeping watch on shell-cratered ground (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). as on open ground: the trench is the cover (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / in-trench / wood**: 0.15 — Share of fire that still hurts a man in a trench, firing or keeping watch in a wood (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a trench in a wood adds the trees' screen against observation (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / in-trench / village**: 0.15 — Share of fire that still hurts a man in a trench, firing or keeping watch in a village (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a trench among ruins, often with cellars close by (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / in-trench / trench**: 0.15 — Share of fire that still hurts a man in a trench, firing or keeping watch on trench ground (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a trench inside a trench system, with traverses and communication trenches to move along (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / in-dugout / open**: 0.02 — Share of fire that still hurts a man down in a dugout on open ground (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a dugout is proof against all but heavy shells landing on it, which the bombardment model handles separately by dugout depth; direct fire cannot reach men below ground (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / in-dugout / cratered**: 0.02 — Share of fire that still hurts a man down in a dugout on shell-cratered ground (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). as on open ground (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / in-dugout / wood**: 0.02 — Share of fire that still hurts a man down in a dugout in a wood (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). as on open ground (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / in-dugout / village**: 0.02 — Share of fire that still hurts a man down in a dugout in a village (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). cellars as dugouts: as on open ground (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **cover / in-dugout / trench**: 0.02 — Share of fire that still hurts a man down in a dugout on trench ground (1 is no protection). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). as on open ground (order of magnitude; tables.md, Cover) |
| **channels / buried-cable / delayMin**: 5 — Minutes a message takes from sender to receiver by buried cable, including writing, sending and delivering it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). telephone or telegraph through an exchange: minutes to get the line and the person; the corps buried cable routes up to brigade and battalion headquarters (boh-1917-1 p. 193) |
| **channels / buried-cable / failPerHour**: 0.02 — Chance per hour that the buried cable link is broken or a message on it is lost. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). buried cable was "proof against direct hits by all calibres up to 5.9-inch howitzers" (boh-1917-1 p. 193): it fails rarely, by a heavy hit or a cut at an exchange |
| **channels / surface-line / delayMin**: 5 — Minutes a message takes from sender to receiver by surface line, including writing, sending and delivering it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). as buried cable once the line is through |
| **channels / surface-line / failPerHour**: 0.3 — Chance per hour that the surface line link is broken or a message on it is lost. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). air lines and lines along trench sides are cut by any shelling; boh-1917-1 p. 193 says cable was more reliable only "provided that the cable could be protected from artillery fire and from traffic". Under shellfire about one hour in three |
| **channels / runner / delayMin**: 30 — Minutes a message takes from sender to receiver by runner, including writing, sending and delivering it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a runner covers 1-2 km between headquarters through trenches, mud and shelling in 20-40 minutes; at Vimy the news of the Schwaben Tunnel exit took an hour to reach the battalion headquarters at its other end (boh-1917-1 p. 325 n.1) |
| **channels / runner / failPerHour**: 0.1 — Chance per hour that the runner link is broken or a message on it is lost. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). runners are killed, wounded or lose their way; one in ten messages an hour lost under fire, more in heavy shelling (at Hill 70 two runners had to go back "through an intense barrage", one was killed, nicholson-1962 p. 291) |
| **channels / visual / delayMin**: 10 — Minutes a message takes from sender to receiver by visual signalling (lamp, flag or shutter), including writing, sending and delivering it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a short message spelt out by lamp or flag and read and relayed: about 10 minutes |
| **channels / visual / failPerHour**: 0.3 — Chance per hour that the visual signalling (lamp, flag or shutter) link is broken or a message on it is lost. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). lamps and flags need line of sight; smoke, mist, snow and dust from the bombardment break it often |
| **channels / pigeon / delayMin**: 40 — Minutes a message takes from sender to receiver by pigeon, including writing, sending and delivering it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the bird flies fast but to a loft well behind, from which the message is telephoned on: 30-60 minutes |
| **channels / pigeon / failPerHour**: 0.1 — Chance per hour that the pigeon link is broken or a message on it is lost. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). birds are killed or stray, and are grounded at night and in heavy weather |
| **channels / power-buzzer / delayMin**: 8 — Minutes a message takes from sender to receiver by power buzzer, including writing, sending and delivering it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). an earth-induction Morse set: a short message in a few minutes, slower than speech |
| **channels / power-buzzer / failPerHour**: 0.2 — Chance per hour that the power buzzer link is broken or a message on it is lost. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the sets were new and few (three to a division forecast for Arras, "no evidence that this was done in every case", boh-1917-1 p. 193 n.2); short range, easily damaged |
| **channels / wireless / delayMin**: 10 — Minutes a message takes from sender to receiver by wireless, including writing, sending and delivering it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). coded Morse through a forward station: about 10 minutes |
| **channels / wireless / failPerHour**: 0.3 — Chance per hour that the wireless link is broken or a message on it is lost. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). boh-1917-1 p. 193: "Valuable as was on occasion the modern field wireless-telegraphy apparatus ... telegraphy or telephony over cables was more reliable"; aerials are shot away and sets break |
| **channels / contact-patrol / delayMin**: 20 — Minutes a message takes from sender to receiver by contact patrol aircraft, including writing, sending and delivering it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the infantry light flares or show panels, the aircraft notes them and drops a message at headquarters or sends it by wireless (nicholson-1962 p. 155): 15-30 minutes |
| **channels / contact-patrol / failPerHour**: 0.2 — Chance per hour that the contact patrol aircraft link is broken or a message on it is lost. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). needs flying weather and the infantry to show their positions; low cloud and snow ground the aircraft |
| **effects / killedFrac**: 0.25 — Share of casualties that are killed (or die of wounds) rather than wounded. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the Canadian Army lost 51,748 killed in action or died of wounds among 232,494 casualties of all kinds (nicholson-1962 p. 535), 22 per cent; the total includes sick and injured, so the battle share of killed is a little higher: 0.25 |
| **effects / officerExposure**: 1.5 — How much more likely than a rifleman an officer is to be hit (multiplies officers / men). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). officers led from the front and were picked out; an officer is taken to be half again as likely to be hit as a man of his company. Order of magnitude; Plan 4 can check it against the officer shares of the casualty returns |
| **effects / densityRefMen**: 30 — Men in a target group at which fire reaches full effect; smaller groups are hit proportionally less. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). about a platoon: SS 143 sets a platoon at 28 to 44 other ranks (ss143-1917 p. 9). A burst or a traverse finds as many men in any group at least that large; smaller groups, a section or a gun team, offer proportionally fewer |
| **effects / suppressionHalfLifeMin**: 5 — Minutes for suppression to halve when no fire is coming in. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). men who have gone to ground under fire take some minutes to get up and go on once it stops; half in 5 minutes, nearly all in 15 |
| **effects / cutPerRound**: 20 — Square metres of wire belt cut by one shell. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a shrapnel or graze-fuzed burst on a belt of wire breaks the strands over a patch of about 4 m by 5 m; so a 20 m deep belt needs about one round per metre of front to be 63 per cent cut. Allenby's planners reckoned on cutting 40 per cent of the wire in a short bombardment (boh-1917-1 p. 179), the same order |
| **effects / damagePerRound**: 5 — Metres of trench wrecked by one shell. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a medium or heavy shell bursting in or beside a trench blows in some 3 to 6 m of it; so one round per 5 m wrecks 63 per cent of a trench |
| **effects / dugoutPerRound**: 10 — Square metres of dugout (segment length times depth) broken by one heavy shell. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a heavy shell with a delay fuze breaks a dugout only by a near-direct hit over it, and the deeper the dugout the less it does: at 10 m depth one round breaks 1 m of a segment's dugouts, at 5 m two. Low on purpose: "no amount of metal or explosive from above could destroy the main German dug-outs ... twenty to fifty feet down in the chalk" (boh-1917-1 p. 316) |
| **effects / safeDepthM**: 3 — Dugout depth in metres at which a garrison is half as exposed to bombardment as one with no dugouts. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the share of a garrison exposed to bombardment is 1 - d / (d + safeDepthM): 3 m of cover halves it, a deep chalk dugout of 6-15 m (20 to 50 ft, boh-1917-1 p. 316) leaves a third to a sixth exposed, for the men who must keep sentries and come up |
| **effects / lossRate**: 0.5 — Share of a garrison lost in a trench that is completely wrecked and has no dugouts. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a garrison without dugouts in a trench that is completely wrecked loses about half its men killed or wounded over a long bombardment; the rest find shell holes or slip away |
| **effects / batteryRecoveryHalfLifeMin**: 120 — Minutes for a silenced battery to recover half of its lost fire. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a battery silenced by counter-battery fire and gas must replace men, horses and ammunition and repair or move its guns: hours, not minutes. Gassed horses kept German batteries from moving or bringing up ammunition at Vimy (boh-1917-1 p. 320) |
| **effects / mudSpeedFactor**: 0.6 — Walking-speed multiplier on mud. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). deep mud slows a loaded man to about half his pace; "the heavy mud slackened their pace" (boh-1917-1 p. 333) |
| **effects / wireSpeedFactor**: 0.5 — Walking-speed multiplier through uncut wire. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). passing through cut or partly cut wire means finding gaps and picking a way through: about half speed |
| **effects / uphillPenaltyPer10m**: 0.02 — Fraction of walking speed lost per 10 metres of climb. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a loaded man climbing a slope loses about 2 per cent of his speed for every 10 m of height gained over a leg; the ridge's slopes rise some tens of metres |
| **effects / nightVisibilityM**: 50 — How far men can see on a moonless night, in metres. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). on a moonless night a man is seen at a few tens of metres; flares light patches for a moment |
| **effects / twilightMin**: 35 — Minutes of twilight at dawn and dusk when visibility rises or falls. | estimate | estimate | civil twilight (sun 0.8 to 6 degrees below the horizon) lasts about 34 minutes at 50.4 deg N in early April (spherical astronomy, declination about +7.6 deg) |
| **effects / tankBogPerKm**: 0.3 — Chance a tank bogs or breaks down per kilometre of cratered ground. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the 1917 Mark II tanks were slow, unreliable and sank in shell craters and mud; about a third bog or break down for each kilometre of cratered ground. Order of magnitude, not fitted to Vimy, where the tanks' fate is a validation target |
| **effects / horseLossPerGasRound**: 0.01 — Fraction of horses within the gas radius lost per gas shell. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). each gas shell bursting within gasHorseRadiusM of a battery's horse lines kills or disables about 1 per cent of them; 100 shells take about 60 per cent. Gassed horses were what kept the German guns from moving at Vimy (boh-1917-1 p. 320) |
| **effects / barrageBandDepthM**: 90 — Depth in metres of the band a creeping barrage covers at any moment. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). about one lift: the Vimy barrage lifted 100 yards (91 m) at a time (nicholson-1962 p. 253), and the zone its shells fall in at any moment is about that deep |
| **effects / mgBarrageDepthM**: 400 — Depth in metres of the zone an indirect machine-gun barrage covers. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the plan's figure, from the machine-gun barrage that created "a bullet-swept zone 400 yards ahead" of the infantry (nicholson-1962 p. 253); the source gives where the zone lay, not its depth, so the depth is an estimate |
| **effects / captureRadiusM**: 50 — Distance in metres within which attackers can capture a trench segment. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). bomb and bayonet range: a Mills bomb is thrown some 30 m and a rush covers the rest; inside 50 m the fight for a trench is hand to hand |
| **effects / captureRatio**: 3 — Attackers to defenders needed to capture a segment (3 means 3 to 1). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the rule of thumb of three attackers to one defender to take a defended position |
| **effects / sortieDelayMin**: 30 — Minutes before defenders sortie from their dugouts after attackers have passed. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). Germans left in dugouts the assault had passed came out once they judged it gone: at Vimy "some sharp combats were still taking place with parties of the enemy emerging from dug-outs which had been passed over" during the consolidation of the Black and Red Lines (boh-1917-1 p. 333), some tens of minutes after |
| **effects / lineFeatureMatchM**: 150 — Distance in metres within which a trench feature counts as belonging to a line. | estimate | estimate | a tolerance for matching a digitized trench to an objective line: the lines and trenches are digitized separately from different maps, a few tens of metres apart at worst, and parallel trenches of one system lie 100-200 m apart |
| **effects / callForFireMin**: 10 — Minutes the guns that answer a call for fire keep firing on its target. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a call for fire was answered with a short burst on the place named, not with a new programme: about 10 minutes of fire, as long as the standing barrage a stalled attack asks for (stallBarrageMin). The call's journey to the guns is the channels' delay, not part of this |
| **effects / stallBarrageMin**: 10 — Minutes a stalled attack waits before calling for a standing barrage on its front. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a stalled attack has lost its barrage (which moves 100 yards every 3 minutes, nicholson-1962 p. 253, so 300 m in 10 minutes) and asks for a standing barrage on its front |
| **effects / gunCaptureRadiusM**: 300 — Distance in metres within which attackers capture a battery. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a battery and its crews occupy about 100 m; attackers within effective rifle and Lewis range (300 m) make it untenable and the guns are taken or abandoned |
| **effects / tankSuppressRadiusM**: 100 — Distance in metres within which a tank suppresses machine guns and infantry. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a tank draws and returns fire within a hundred metres; machine-gun posts that see it close up go quiet |
| **effects / gasHorseRadiusM**: 200 — Distance in metres from the point of impact within which gas reaches horses. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a gas shell's cloud spreads and drifts a couple of hundred metres before it thins; the plan's 1 km would gas whole valleys from one shell, so 200 m |
| **effects / gapMultiplier**: 2 — How many times the doctrine's follow distance a company must lag behind the barrage before its losses are put down to the barrage having moved on. | estimate | estimate | an explanation threshold, not behaviour: a company more than twice its follow distance behind the barrage is said to have lost it (cause barrage-moved-on) |
| **effects / maxTargetsPerFirer**: 2 — Most targets a single firer can engage at once. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a company engages the nearest enemies; splitting its fire between more than two groups is unrealistic for one company commander's control |
| **effects / crewPerMg**: 4 — Men who serve one machine gun (they fire no rifle). | estimate | estimate | the men at the gun in action: the firer, the loader and two ammunition carriers. The German machine-gun company had 48 gunners for 6 guns, 8 a gun (german-handbook-1917 p. 50), the others reliefs and carriers |
| **effects / trenchProximityM**: 15 — Distance in metres within which a company counts as being in a trench. | estimate | estimate | a trench, its parapet and the shell holes along it lie within a few metres of the digitized line, which is itself a few metres off |
| **effects / churnAreaPerShellM2**: 30 — Square metres of ground one shell churns up. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a field-gun or howitzer shell leaves a crater a few metres across (about 3 to 8 m) and throws up a lip around it; about 30 square metres of ground disturbed a round is the middle of that range. Order of magnitude, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / crateredShare**: 0.5 — Share of a 50 m square covered by shell holes at which its ground counts as cratered. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). once shell holes and their lips cover about half of a 50 m cell, no line across it is unbroken and men move and shelter as on cratered ground. A threshold of judgement, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / wireUncutBelow**: 0.5 — Share of a trench segment's wire cut below which the bombardment's result gives the cause wire-uncut. | estimate | estimate | an explanation threshold, not a rate. With half a belt still standing the gaps are lanes the enemy's machine guns cover, and the attackers must look for them or cut a way through; below that share cut, the wire is said to be uncut. A judgement, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / strongpointRadiusM**: 100 — Radius in metres of a strong point mapped as a point with no radius of its own; its diameter is the frontage the bombardment allots rounds by. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a strong point held by a company or more is taken to hold about one company's frontage of trench: the scenario's 41 first-position garrison companies hold some 8 km of it between them, about 200 m each (oob-german.yaml, geo/features.geojson), so a diameter of 200 m. Geometry by judgement, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / villageRadiusM**: 50 — Radius in metres of a village mapped as a point with no radius of its own. | estimate | estimate | the radius the map has drawn a village with when it gives none (packages/app/src/map/style.ts DEFAULT_RADIUS_M), a hamlet's few houses; every Vimy village is mapped with its own radius |
| **effects / woodRadiusM**: 60 — Radius in metres of a wood mapped as a point with no radius of its own. | estimate | estimate | the radius the map has drawn a wood with when it gives none (packages/app/src/map/style.ts DEFAULT_RADIUS_M), a copse; the Vimy woods are mapped as polygons |
| **effects / craterRadiusM**: 10 — Radius in metres of a crater mapped as a point with no radius of its own. | estimate | estimate | the radius the map has drawn a crater with when it gives none (packages/app/src/map/style.ts DEFAULT_RADIUS_M); a mine crater of 1916-17 was some tens of metres across, a shell crater a few metres |
| **effects / unobservedFireFactor**: 0.3 — How effective the fire of a battery observed by forward observers or aircraft is when nobody can see where it falls, as a share of observed fire (fire by the map on positions plotted in advance is not reduced). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a battery that should be corrected by an observer but whose fall of shot nobody sees fires by the map at a target it has not registered, and most of its rounds land off it; about a third as effective as observed fire. Order of magnitude, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / observerRangeM**: 1500 — Distance in metres an artillery observer must be able to see to watch the fall of shot; observed fire loses effect in proportion when visibility is shorter. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a forward observer in an observation post, or an aircraft observer over the lines, watches shells fall on targets one to two kilometres off; 1,500 m is the middle. When men can see less far than that (night, mist, snow) observation falls in proportion. Order of magnitude, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / batteriesRecoveredFrac**: 0.5 — Share of a side's guns in action at which the event batteries-recovered is emitted, and its defensive fire carries that cause. | estimate | estimate | an explanation threshold, not behaviour. Once half of a side's guns are back in action after the counter-battery fire at Zero, its defensive fire is said to have recovered (cause batteries-recovered) |
| **effects / blockedExitFactor**: 1 — How much a wrecked trench slows its garrison coming up out of its dug-outs; the time to emerge is emergeMin x (1 + blockedExitFactor x trenchDamage). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). shelling that wrecks a trench also buries and blocks dug-out stairways, and the garrison must dig or climb out through the wreckage; a wholly wrecked trench is taken to double the time to come up. Order of magnitude, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / isolationRadiusM**: 300 — Distance in metres within which a company needs another company of its side, when an enemy is that close, not to count as isolated (doctrine isolationDrain then lowers its morale). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a company with no other company of its side near it while the enemy is near is cut off: it cannot see its neighbours or be supported by them. A company holds about 200 m of front (strongpointRadiusM), so 300 m reaches past the middle of the next company's frontage on either side. A judgement, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / mgUnobservedBelow**: 0.2 — Trench damage below which a garrison's machine guns opening fire are said to come from a segment the bombardment did not deal with (cause mg-unobserved). | estimate | estimate | an explanation threshold, not a rate. A segment whose trench the bombardment wrecked less than a fifth of, or that was spared on purpose, kept its machine-gun posts and their fields of fire, so the guns' first fire is said to come from posts the bombardment did not find (cause mg-unobserved). A judgement, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / fireRefsMin**: 10 — Minutes of earlier fire on a company that its morale events refer to (refs) and take their fire causes from. | estimate | estimate | an explanation window, not behaviour. A company pinned, falling back, surrendering or destroyed is said to have been brought to it by the fire of the last 10 minutes, about three barrage lifts; earlier fire is reached through the refs of that fire |
| **effects / suppressionSpeedLoss**: 0.5 — Share of its walking speed a fully suppressed company loses; its speed is multiplied by 1 - suppressionSpeedLoss x suppression. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the plan's starting value: men under fire move from cover to cover in short rushes and stop to return fire, so a company fully suppressed but not pinned keeps about half its pace. A judgement, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / fatiguePerHourUnderFire**: 0.1 — Fatigue a company gains for each hour that fire comes in on it (on the doctrine's 0 to 1 scale, beside fatiguePerKm). | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). time under fire tires men as marching does: crouching, digging in and the strain of shelling. An hour of it is taken to tire like 5 km of advance with a full load (doctrine fatiguePerKm 0.02 a km), so 0.1 an hour. A judgement, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / bombsPerManCleared**: 0.5 — Bombs (grenades) a mopping-up company uses for each man it clears out of the dugouts. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). moppers-up bomb a dugout's entrances and call on the men below to come up (ss143-1917 p. 16: they 'guard the entrances to dugouts'); a party of 20 to 40 men is taken to come up after some 10 to 20 bombs down its stairways, about one bomb for every two men. A judgement, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / runnerMinPerKm**: 15 — Minutes a runner needs for each kilometre between the sender and the receiver beyond the runnerDelayCoversKm that the runner channel's delayMin already allows for; nothing is added for a shorter way. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a man on foot through trenches, shell holes and mud covers about 4 km an hour, so some 15 minutes a kilometre. The owner's research compendium has runners' times growing with the distance they go (a design estimate there too). A judgement, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / runnerDelayCoversKm**: 1.5 — Kilometres of a runner's way that the runner channel's delayMin already allows for; runnerMinPerKm applies only to the distance beyond it. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). the runner channel's delayMin (30 minutes) was reasoned as a runner covering 1-2 km between headquarters in 20-40 minutes, so it already allows for about 1.5 km; only the way beyond that adds runnerMinPerKm |
| **effects / callForFireGuns**: 6 — Guns that answer a call for fire, taken from the nearest batteries of the caller's side that are in action. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a call for fire is taken to be answered by one battery, taken as six guns for both sides (the British field battery as commonly given, not checked against a source read for this project). A judgement, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / hqRearOffsetBattalionM**: 600 — Metres behind the middle of its companies' places at Zero (away from the enemy) at which a battalion headquarters stands when the scenario gives no hqPosition. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a battalion headquarters stood in its support or reserve trench, a dugout or a tunnel exit, some hundreds of metres behind its companies at Zero. A judgement, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / hqRearOffsetBrigadeM**: 2000 — Metres behind the middle of its companies' places at Zero (away from the enemy) at which a brigade or regiment headquarters stands when the scenario gives no hqPosition. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a brigade (or German regiment) headquarters stood a kilometre or two behind the front line, beyond field-gun reach of most trench fire. A judgement, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / hqRearOffsetDivisionM**: 6000 — Metres behind the middle of its companies' places at Zero (away from the enemy) at which a division headquarters stands when the scenario gives no hqPosition. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a division headquarters stood some kilometres back, in a village or chateau beyond most of the enemy's field artillery. A judgement, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / hqRearOffsetCorpsM**: 12000 — Metres behind the middle of its companies' places at Zero (away from the enemy) at which a corps or group headquarters stands when the scenario gives no hqPosition. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). a corps (or German Gruppe) headquarters stood about ten kilometres or more behind the front, beyond the reach of all but the heaviest guns. A judgement, not fitted to Vimy |
| **effects / hqRearOffsetArmyM**: 25000 — Metres behind the middle of its companies' places at Zero (away from the enemy) at which an army headquarters stands when the scenario gives no hqPosition. | estimate | estimate | calibrated (Plan 4). an army headquarters stood a day's march or more behind the front. A judgement, not fitted to Vimy |

## Open questions for the historian

1. [vimy/bombardment.md] BOH's ammunition table (vol. I, p. 315 n.2), row by row. The OCR loses the heavy rows (Reading the ammunition table). A clean scan or the printed page would give each heavy piece's rounds by phase and by demolition or counter-battery, in place of the estimates here. The archive.org page images are served from a host outside the allowed list (checked 2026-11-21), so they were not read.
2. [vimy/bombardment.md] How the field guns' bombardment rounds divided between wire-cutting, keeping demolished works under fire and night harassing fire. The split entered, half, a quarter and a quarter, is a guess. The divisional artillery programmes or Canadian Corps Artillery Instruction No. 1 would settle it.
3. [vimy/bombardment.md] How many of the howitzers' rounds cut wire with the 106 fuze (App. 15, para. 8(h)). None is entered as wire.
4. [vimy/bombardment.md] The breadth of the wire in front of the first position and the Zwischen-Stellung. 15 yards is borrowed from the Hindenburg Line. The second belt in front of the Second Line is taken as broad as the first.
5. [vimy/bombardment.md] The spared stretch's extent. How long was the stretch of trench opposite the 87th left unbombarded, and where did it lie in the 87th's frontage? The band entered is the right half of the frontage. The 87th's war diary or a trench map of Hill 145 would place it. Was any other stretch spared for the same reason?
6. [vimy/bombardment.md] German machine guns at Vimy. Was the resting battalions' machine-gun company in the line, as entered, or with its battalion? Did the 79th Reserve, 1st Bavarian Reserve and 16th Bavarian Divisions have a machine-gun marksman detachment on the ridge? How many 08/15 light machine guns had reached the companies by 9 April? The regimental histories BOH cites (p. 352 n.2), or Dieterich's article in *Canadian Military History* 15:1, would answer it. scholars.wlu.ca served a Cloudflare challenge in earlier checks, so the article was not read.
7. [vimy/bombardment.md] Dug-out depths by position. The 30 and 50 feet entered are points within BOH's "twenty to fifty feet". German engineer records or post-battle surveys of the captured dug-outs would give real depths.
8. [vimy/bombardment.md] Officers in action. What was the CEF infantry company's war establishment of officers in 1917, and how many officers did a Canadian company take into the attack on 9 April? Was the second-in-command left out of battle? How many Lewis guns did each battalion actually have? Company-specific figures would replace the doctrine defaults.
9. [vimy/bombardment.md] Ammunition for the battle. The heavy batteries', the mortars' and the German batteries' stocks on 9 April. The 1,300 rounds a gun is the 5th Division's artillery, applied to every field gun.
10. [vimy/bombardment.md] Battery positions. Map 7 marks divisional, medium and heavy batteries and super-heavy guns with separate symbols. A census of them, by class, would place each artillery entry better than the one point from BOH's distances. The symbols are too small and too close to the map's red lettering for the colour-component method used for the frame's extent. Also: where were the German batteries of Group Vimy, battery by battery or by group, beyond the casemates in the eastern woods? As entered, the 197 German pieces stand at two points, so a run that takes Farbus Wood and the Bois du Goulot captures all of them at once, against the 54 guns actually taken in six days (Artillery).
11. [vimy/bombardment.md] Traction of the heavy pieces. Which heavy and siege batteries were horse-drawn and which tractor-drawn?
12. [vimy/command.md] The divisions' signal schemes. The corps scheme says each division worked out its own visual system, and the 1st, 3rd and 4th Divisions' instructions were not read (only the 2nd's, in BOH's appendices). Did battalion headquarters reach the buried cable during 9 April, and how fast did reports from battalions reach brigade?
13. [vimy/command.md] Field artillery liaison. Did the Canadian brigades and battalions have artillery liaison officers at Vimy, and did a brigade have a direct call on its supporting batteries, as the British 3rd Division's brigades had?
14. [vimy/command.md] The German links. How did the 79th Reserve and 1st Bavarian Reserve Divisions' regiments report to their divisions, and the divisions to Gruppe Vimy, on 9 April? Was there buried cable below division?
15. [vimy/command.md] The German defence orders. Did Gruppe Vimy or its divisions issue standing counter-attack orders for the regiments' support and resting battalions (the Gegenstoss of the 1916 defensive principles)? If so, they are planned operations that this note does not have.
16. [vimy/command.md] The 75 per cent. What is the source of the brief's "about 75% of counter-battery shoots" adjusted from the air? The Canadian Corps' counter-battery records, or an article in *Canadian Military History* (`scholars.wlu.ca`, which serves a challenge page, so it was not read)?
17. [vimy/command.md] The XVII Corps' guns on the flank. How many XVII Corps guns fired on the Canadian right on 9 April, and on what?
18. [vimy/command.md] The I Corps' artillery after Zero. Which I Corps batteries neutralized the hostile artillery north of the Canadian front after Zero, and should they stand off the map rather than in the Canadian artillery park?
19. [vimy/command.md] The crest as key ground. Is holding Hill 135 and Hill 145 a fair test of holding the crest, or should a crest line (for example along La Folie Farm between them) be digitized?
20. [vimy/command.md] The 5th Division's role. Were the British 15th and 95th Brigades ever ordered to prepare for Vimy and Willerval before the battle, beyond First Army's appreciation?
21. [vimy/command.md] When the heavy guns were up on the ridge. When did the first Canadian Corps heavy batteries come into action on or beyond the crest? The appreciation made the occupation of Willerval wait for them; the scenario takes the evening of 12 April, the earliest reading of BOH's "some days", but the Canadians were still using captured guns on 14 April.
22. [vimy/command.md] The 24th Division's date (for the owner). The Bois en Hache attack is scheduled on 12 April, the date it was made after the postponement, though the plan had it on the night of 9-10 April with the Pimple. Should a scheduled flank attack keep the historical date, take the planned one, or be tied to the Pimple operation when the model allows it?
23. [vimy/fireplan.md] Group Vimy's guns by type. BOH p. 353 gives the 79th Reserve and 1st Bavarian Reserve Divisions' 89 heavy and 108 field pieces without a split by calibre; they are entered as the representative 15-cm howitzer and 77-mm field gun (Weapon ids). A German source (the divisions' artillery orders, *Die Osterschlacht bei Arras*) would give the mix.
24. [vimy/fireplan.md] The divisional barrage maps and Canadian Corps Artillery Instruction No. 1 (35 pages, `rca-ww1-history`) would give each division's real lifts, the standing times on intermediate trenches and the protective barrage's distance and duration beyond each final objective (`standOffM`, the final `pauseMin`). None was found on the allowed hosts.
25. [vimy/fireplan.md] "85% of 175 located". The plan's counter-battery dispute pairs Nicholson's 83% of 212 with "85% of 175 located", a figure from the owner's brief. No source read gives it. Candidates: McNaughton's "Counter Battery Work" (*Canadian Defence Quarterly*, 1926; Nicholson n.54), the C.B.S.O. report Nicholson cites (n.63), or Hogan (below).
26. [vimy/fireplan.md] Canadian Military History, on scholars.wlu.ca. The issue pages load, and three relevant articles were identified: B. Hogan, "Operational Research and Counter-Battery Fires in the Canadian Corps, 1917-18" (CMH 32:2, 2023); A. Dieterich, "The German 79th Reserve Infantry Division in the Battle of Vimy Ridge, April 1917" (CMH 15:1, 2006); H. Herwig, "'The Battle-Fortune of Marshal Hindenburg is not Bound up with the Possession of a Hill': The Germans and Vimy Ridge, April 1917" (CMH 25:2, 2016). Their PDFs (`cgi/viewcontent.cgi`) and the site search return the Cloudflare challenge (HTTP 403, checked 2026-11-20 18:40 UTC), so they were not read and not worked around.
27. [vimy/fireplan.md] The RCA fire-plan essay named in the owner's brief was not found on rca-arc.org (site search for "vimy", the Brooks essay pages, the heritage history page). The heritage page used here is a different, shorter text.
28. [vimy/fireplan.md] The heavy pieces by task. Which pieces were in the counter-battery groups; the roles here are inferred from the weapon type.
29. [vimy/fireplan.md] Nicholson's 983. What it counts: whether it includes the 13-pdrs, which mortars, and whether the I Corps pieces are outside it (they are taken to be). The medium-mortar count, 76, is the remainder and would change with it.
30. [vimy/fireplan.md] Battery size. The silent forward batteries' guns (48) assume three 18-pdr batteries in each of the 30 field brigades.
31. [vimy/geometry.md] Digitizing scripts were not committed. The traces were made with scratch scripts (least-cost tracing of the front lines, gridded crops and hand-read vertices for the rest) that are not in the repository; only the fit and overlay code (`tools/georef`) is. The geometry can be checked against Map 7 with the overlays but not regenerated.
32. [vimy/geometry.md] The red-4cdn and black-4cdn lines nearly touch (about 3.5 m apart at y ≈ 789, where the estimated section begins). They are both estimates from the text there; the 12th Brigade's real Red objective may lie further on.
33. [vimy/geometry.md] Subway names. Map 7 draws twelve subway outlines in the Canadian lines without names, and Chapter VIII names none (only Zivy Cave). Goodman, Grange, Lichfield and the others need a trench map or a tunnelling-company history to tie names to outlines. Nicholson counts eleven subways (p. 250) and six in the 4th Division's sector (p. 259); the map shows twelve and five.
34. [vimy/geometry.md] Mine craters. Map 7 shows none, and no source read for the geometry gives crater coordinates that can be tied to a named crater. The crater groups in no man's land (from trench maps, or the memorial park's preserved craters) are missing. The searches tried (all 2026-11-20, UTC): Map 7's legend and symbology: no crater symbol; the green rings are orchards (above). `en.wikipedia.org` search API, query "Vimy crater" (16:54): titles Battle of Vimy Ridge, Canadian National Vimy Memorial, Vimy, German attack on Vimy Ridge, Tunnelling companies of the Royal Engineers, Thélus, 172nd and 176th Tunnelling Company. Not read: the next queries ("Vimy mine crater coordinates", "Broadmarsh crater", "Grange subway Vimy", the same on `de.wikipedia.org`, and a 3 km geosearch around the memorial) were refused with HTTP 429 (rate limit), and retries with backoff over about two minutes were refused too. Articles "Lichfield Crater" and "Zivy Crater" do not exist on en.wikipedia.org (16:58, `prop=coordinates`). `commons.wikimedia.org` geosearch, files within 2.5 km of the memorial (281 files): no title containing crater, krater, cratère or entonnoir. IGN BD TOPO V3 WFS (`data.geopf.fr`), bounding box 2.70-2.86° E, 50.33-50.41° N: `detail_orographique` (30 objects: valleys, summits, ridges, a slope, a spoil heap; no crater), `ligne_orographique` (banks, levees, quarries), `zone_d_activite_ou_d_interet` (no crater), `toponymie` (584 names) and `cimetiere` (38). One lead. BD TOPO's `cimetiere` layer has two Commonwealth war cemeteries, both named "Lichfield Crater": `CIMETIER0000000310100058` centred at 2.776652° E, 50.359575° N (frame −358, −2173) and `CIMETIER0000000219989140` at 2.778135° E, 50.354721° N (frame −395, −2718). Both lie on the Canadian front line as digitized, in the 2nd Division's sector: the first 27 m east of `cdn-front`, the second 8 m west of it (within the fit's error). But BD TOPO gives both the same name, so which crater each is cannot be settled from the allowed hosts; neither was entered. Should they be entered as unnamed crater features?
35. [vimy/geometry.md] The 12th Brigade's objectives north of the Black and Red bands are estimates (above). Is there a trench map or order giving them?
36. [vimy/geometry.md] Blue and Brown north of Thélus are estimates from the text. A barrage map or trench map would fix them.
37. [vimy/geometry.md] brown-2cdn's northern section lies inside the barrage's stand-off beyond blue-2cdn. On the loaded lines the Brown Line is only 43 m beyond the Blue at y −1921, 55 m at y −1952, 99 m at y −2072 and 128 m at y −2200 (measured along x). The fire plan halts the barrage 180 m beyond each objective (`standOffM`, fireplan.md) and companies follow 70 m behind it (`followDistanceM`, doctrine), so they may close up to 110 m beyond the Blue Line. That section is one of the text-based estimates above (Blue and Brown north of Thélus). It causes an artefact in runs: where Brown is less than 110 m beyond Blue, companies closing up behind the barrage halted on the Blue Line cross the Brown Line early. In the seed-1 run six companies (2kosb-c, 14rwarwick-b and -c, 15rwarwick-b and -c, 1rwk-d) log "reaches Brown Line, 2nd Canadian Division" at 10:46-10:50, at the start of the 96-minute halt on the Blue Line, more than 1.5 hours before the barrage moves on and 2.5 hours before the timetable's Brown Line (13:18). Read those early "reaches" as a geometry artefact, not a result. The geometry and the engine were left unchanged; does Map 7, a barrage map or a trench map put the Brown Line further east north of y ≈ −2200?
38. [vimy/geometry.md] Division boundaries are from the battalion arrows; the Corps' boundary orders would be better.
39. [vimy/geometry.md] Five red dots west of x −6000 (around Villers-au-Bois and Mont-St-Éloi): are they batteries, and should the extent reach them when batteries are placed on the map? (The artillery park is entered by role and count, not placed.)
40. [vimy/geometry.md] Trench maps (1:10,000 or 1:20,000) remain the way to sharpen the trench geometry; none was reachable from the allowed hosts during the geometry research.
41. [vimy/historical.md] `gunsCaptured.field` holds guns of all calibres. The schema has no other slot, so the field-gun count entered holds the guns of every calibre the sources give (heavy and field together); it is not a count of field guns only.
42. [vimy/historical.md] Captured guns, "54 or 63". The spec's lead gives 63 as an alternative; no source read gives it. Possibly Duguid, "Canada on Vimy Ridge" (*Canada Year Book*, 1936), which Nicholson cites (n.67, n.85), or the Canadian Corps Report on Operations.
43. [vimy/historical.md] BOH's casualty tables. The 3rd, 8th and 11th Brigades' rows (pp. 339 n.1, 342 n.1), the 7th Brigade's row (entered as estimates from the builder's reading of the OCR; a page-image check would make them sourced), and the 12 April battalion table (p. 346 n.2) need checking against the printed page or a page image. archive.org's page images are served from storage hosts outside the allowed list.
44. [vimy/historical.md] black-4cdn's latest bound (04:15 on 10 April) is the 11th Brigade's third trench beyond the summit (BOH p. 342). The 12th Brigade, in the same sector, held only parts of its second and third trenches on the 9th, "improved their positions next morning" and moved up after the Hangstellung fell (Nicholson p. 261; BOH p. 343), so the window may close too early for the sector's northern half.
45. [vimy/historical.md] Division totals. Only the 2nd Division's can be added up (an estimate); the 1st's lacks the 3rd Brigade, the 3rd's the 8th, the 4th's the 11th and the 10th Brigade's 10 and 12 April losses.
46. [vimy/historical.md] Battalion losses. The spec's lead that the 87th and 102nd "each lost over 300 men on 9 April" is not in either official history; the battalions' war diaries or histories would settle it.
47. [vimy/historical.md] German losses for the 16th Bavarian Division and the attached battalions, and German figures by day, are not in the sources read; the scholarly German-side articles in *Canadian Military History* (Dieterich 2006; Herwig 2016) are behind scholars.wlu.ca's Cloudflare challenge (fireplan.md, Open questions); Sheldon's *The German Army at Vimy Ridge* was not read.
48. [vimy/historical.md] Reserve arrival times are estimates from "late afternoon", "after dusk" and "early on the 10th". The German regimental histories and *Die Osterschlacht bei Arras 1917*, which BOH used (p. 352 n.2), give the hours.
49. [vimy/leaders.md] Byng's moraleEffect 4 rests on his commanders briefing their subordinates, while its own justification says there is no direct evidence of his effect on morale. Owner to confirm or lower.
50. [vimy/leaders.md] Fasbender's adaptiveness 4 rests on tertiary evidence that BOH p. 355 contradicts (it credits Rupprecht and Lossberg with the defensive adaptation). Owner to confirm or lower.
51. [vimy/leaders.md] Wichura's orderSpeed 4 rests on an outcome with no order time; the rubric says a result alone supports a 4 at most. Owner to confirm or lower.
52. [vimy/leaders.md] Battalion commanding officers not found for 26 Canadian battalions: the 1st, 2nd, 4th, 7th, 13th, 14th, 16th, 19th, 20th, 38th, 42nd, 43rd, 44th, 47th, 50th, 54th, 60th, 73rd, 87th, 102nd, The RCR, the PPCLI, the 1st, 2nd, 4th and 5th CMR; and for all 12 British battalions. The Canadian battalions' Wikipedia articles (where they exist) have no dated list, and `www.cefresearch.ca` redirects to `cefresearch.ca`, a host the research may not reach. War diaries on `bac-lac.gc.ca` name them; reading them was beyond the leaders research.
53. [vimy/leaders.md] The British brigades' commanders' first names (`L. O. W. Jones`, `M. N. Turner`, `Lord E. Gordon-Lennox`) and the 5th Division's (`R. B. Stephens`): initials only in `hussey-inman-1921`.
54. [vimy/leaders.md] Ratings with no cited conduct (Stephens, Hill, Turner, Gordon-Lennox, and most moraleEffect and riskTolerance ratings): the sources read give nothing to rate. Battalion and brigade war diaries, Cook's and Godefroy's studies, and memoirs would, and the owner may know them.
55. [vimy/leaders.md] Brigade majors and staff, who shaped much of the planning credited here to the commanders, are not recorded.
56. [vimy/oob-canadian.md] The strength estimate's range. 179 a company counts only the 21 first-wave battalions in Nicholson's 15,000. If the supporting battalions that start at Zero are counted in the first wave too, the same total spreads over more companies and comes to about 139 a company. The entered 179 is the upper end of that range; which reading is right needs the war diaries' strengths.
57. [vimy/oob-canadian.md] The 72nd Battalion's place in the line. The battalion's own history (`mcevoy-finlay-1920`) puts it on the left flank of the whole attack, which conflicts with Nicholson's "from right to left the 38th, 72nd and 73rd" (p. 260), the order entered. Which is right?
58. [vimy/oob-canadian.md] Battalion frontages and company dispositions. Map 7's arrows give each assaulting battalion's axis, but are legible only for the 2nd Division at the scan reachable. Brigade operation orders (in the war diaries on `recherche-collection-search.bac-lac.gc.ca`) would give each battalion's frontage, its waves and which companies led. Battalion company letters (A-D) are taken in each battalion's own order; where a battalion numbered its companies, the order of the letters is the builder's.
59. [vimy/oob-canadian.md] Effective strengths at Zero, battalion by battalion (war diaries' "strength going in"), especially for the battalions whose histories are on archive.org but were not read for this (for example the 20th, 25th, 102nd, 4th).
60. [vimy/oob-canadian.md] The 9th Brigade's position on 9 April, and whether the 60th Battalion was still with it.
61. [vimy/oob-canadian.md] The British 5th Division is entered as a `group`, not a `division`. It was a division under Byng's command, in corps reserve with its 15th and 95th Brigades (Nicholson p. 252 note; `hussey-inman-1921` p. 157); its 13th Brigade fought with the 2nd Canadian Division and sits there. It is entered as `kind: group` (`5bdiv`, led by its commander) so that the corps' divisions are the four Canadian ones; nothing else reads the kind. Is that acceptable, or should it be a `division` and the corps' divisions counted another way?
62. [vimy/oob-canadian.md] The British 15th and 95th Brigades' bivouacs lie 0.3-2.9 km west of the frame's extent. Should they be off-map `reserves` entries (with `distanceKm` and an `entryPoint`) instead of companies held at the extent's edge, or should the extent grow (with the terrain grid)?
63. [vimy/oob-canadian.md] The 47th Battalion's task on 9 April (attached to the 11th Brigade, p. 259): not found.
64. [vimy/oob-canadian.md] Which two companies of the 46th attacked the craters on the evening of 9 April (p. 261), and where.
65. [vimy/oob-canadian.md] The 1st and 6th Brigades' assembly areas and start times; only the British 13th Brigade's are sourced.
66. [vimy/oob-canadian.md] The 10th Brigade's position on 9 April while standing by for the Pimple (Zouave Valley?).
67. [vimy/oob-german.md] The tier of `aef-251-divisions`. It is registered as `primary`, but it is a 1919-20 compilation made from Allied intelligence, not German records. Should it be tier `official` (or lower) rather than `primary`? The registry is unchanged.
68. [vimy/oob-german.md] The northern division: the 16th or the 14th Bavarian Division? Entered: the 16th Bavarian Infantry Division in Gruppe Souchez (Nicholson p. 246, citing the Reichsarchiv map, G.O.H. XII, Appx. 8, in n.44; BOH pp. 317 n.2, 353, 355; `aef-251-divisions` p. 273). The owner's brief has the 14th Bavarian, which BOH puts at the other end of Group Vimy, north of the Scarpe (pp. 276, 353); the brief's source for it was not seen. Does any primary source put the 14th Bavarian Division opposite Souchez or the Pimple on 9 April, or is the "14th" the 14th Bavarian *Infantry Regiment* of the 16th Bavarian Division? Appendix 8 of *Der Weltkrieg* vol. 12 would settle it.
69. [vimy/oob-german.md] The German official history and monograph. Reichsarchiv, *Der Weltkrieg 1914 bis 1918*, vol. 12, and *Die Osterschlacht bei Arras 1917* (Schlachten des Weltkrieges vol. 28) are not on archive.org (searched 2026-11-20: only *Der Weltkrieg* vols. 2-3 and *Schlachten des Weltkrieges* vols. 2, 3, 18, 32, 33 are there). They would give every regiment's sector, battalion roles, company strengths and the counter-attack orders' times.
70. [vimy/oob-german.md] The German regimental histories (RIR 261, 262, 263; BRIR 1, 2, 3; BIR 11, 14; BRIR 21) were not found on archive.org. BOH p. 352 n.2 used those of the 261st, 262nd, Bavarian 1st, 2nd Reserve, 11th, 14th and 21st; Nicholson p. 254 n.75 that of the 263rd.
71. [vimy/oob-german.md] The scholarly articles. *Canadian Military History* has "The German 79th Reserve Infantry Division in the Battle of Vimy Ridge, April 1917" (A. Dieterich, vol. 15 iss. 1, 2006, scholars.wlu.ca/cmh/vol15/iss1/7) and "'The Battle-Fortune of Marshal Hindenburg is not Bound up with the Possession of a Hill': The Germans and Vimy Ridge" (H. Herwig, vol. 25 iss. 2, 2016, scholars.wlu.ca/cmh/vol25/iss2/16). Their landing pages were reached, but the PDFs sit behind a bot challenge on scholars.wlu.ca and could not be fetched. Dieterich commanded the 79th Reserve Infantry Brigade (`wikipedia-vimy-oob`); his account is the best source for the 79th's dispositions. Sheldon, *The German Army at Vimy Ridge* / *The German Army in the Spring Offensives 1917*, and Godefroy's chapter in *Vimy Ridge: A Canadian Reassessment* (2007) were not reachable.
72. [vimy/oob-german.md] Regimental boundaries and company sectors. The zones are built from three anchors and equal widths; German or British trench maps (with the "cross-roads on Hill 145") would fix them.
73. [vimy/oob-german.md] The second and third trenches. The map has only the first trench and, in the south, the Zwölfer-Graben and the Zwischen-Stellung; most support companies stand on the first position for want of the third trench. Digitizing the forward zone's second and third trenches (the geometry research's ruling kept the map's features unchanged) would let them stand where they were.
74. [vimy/oob-german.md] Which battalion was in front, in support and resting in each regiment on 9 April, except the four sourced.
75. [vimy/oob-german.md] Company strength at Zero, by regiment, and the source of the brief's 264 and 150.
76. [vimy/oob-german.md] The billets of the resting battalions other than the 262nd's and 263rd's, and which of those two held which village.
77. [vimy/oob-german.md] BIR 14 and BRIR 21 on 9 April. Whether BIR 14 held the Pimple (as entered) and BRIR 21 the line north of the Souchez, or the other way round.
78. [vimy/oob-german.md] The two other counter-attack divisions of the five, and where all five lay on the morning of 9 April; when each was ordered forward.
79. [vimy/oob-german.md] The 111th Division's position on 9 April.
80. [vimy/oob-german.md] Times. BOH gives the German times (11 a.m., 6 p.m.) without saying whether it converted them; they are entered as given, and the scenario's clock is British (BOH p. 318 n.2 marks one German report "(British time)").
81. [vimy/oob-german.md] The German brigade commanders (79th Reserve Infantry Brigade: Dieterich, per `wikipedia-vimy-oob`), and whether a brigade level belongs in the tree.
82. [vimy/sources.md] Is a single attack bearing of 75° acceptable for the whole corps, given that the 4th Division attacked at about 62° and the 2nd at about 87°? If the engine later needs per-division axes, the sectors could carry their own.
83. [vimy/sources.md] The extent stops at the German Third Line (Oppy-Méricourt). Should it reach the partly built Drocourt-Quéant Line (Nicholson's "fourth position", p. 246) if later scenarios cover the advance after 12 April?
84. [vimy/sources.md] Map 7 covers the whole corps front at a small scale and says "Only principal features and those mentioned in the text are shown". Which period trench maps of the ridge can be had from the allowed hosts, to georeference for the trench lines? None has been searched for yet.
85. [vimy/weather.md] War diaries and meteorological records. The brief asks for war diaries; none was read; the Canadian war diaries held by Library and Archives Canada (`recherche-collection-search.bac-lac.gc.ca`) were not searched for the weather research. A First Army or Canadian Corps meteorological section return would give temperatures, wind speeds and times.
86. [vimy/weather.md] 10 April. Neither source describes the weather by day on the 10th, so there is no entry between the clear night of the 9th-10th and the 11th.
87. [vimy/weather.md] 11 April. Only "deteriorated rapidly"; the snow entered for it is an estimate.
88. [model/doctrine.md] SS 135. *Instructions for the Training of Divisions for Offensive Action* (December 1916) would give the distance the leading wave kept behind the barrage, the distances between waves and the mopping-up arrangements of the Canadian attack; it was not found on archive.org. The distance behind the barrage (`followDistanceM` 70 m) is the one the engine uses today.
89. [model/doctrine.md] The Grundsätze, 1 March 1917 edition. Not found on archive.org in German or English. It would give the intended timing of the Gegenstoss and the Gegenangriff, the Eingreif divisions' distance behind the line, and how long a garrison was expected to take to man its trench after the barrage lifted.
90. [model/doctrine.md] SS 143's wave distances. The 50 to 100 yards between waves are read from the OCR of Appendix I's diagram, whose figures are garbled in both scans read; the page image should be checked.
91. [model/doctrine.md] German light machine guns in April 1917. The order of 12 March 1917 gave each company three; how many the Vimy garrisons had by 9 April is not known (taken as one).
92. [model/doctrine.md] German company officers at Vimy. The establishment was five; the handbook says companies were commanded by subalterns. The number actually present (taken as three) is not known.
93. [model/doctrine.md] The morale scale. The morale thresholds have no source. A study of when attacks of 1917 went to ground or broke, by the losses suffered, would anchor them.
94. [model/tables.md] Hogg and Thurston, Farndale, Clarke. Most British ranges and shell weights come from Wikipedia, which cites Hogg and Thurston's *British Artillery Weapons and Ammunition 1914-1918*, Farndale's *History of the Royal Regiment of Artillery: Western Front 1914-18* and Clarke's *British Artillery 1914-19*. None was found on the allowed hosts; the official handbooks of each piece (the "Handbook of the B.L. 9.2-inch howitzer" and the like) would replace them.
95. [model/tables.md] Sustained rates of fire of the heavy pieces. No rate was found for the 60-pdr, the 6-inch gun and howitzer, the 8-inch, 9.2-inch, 12-inch and 15-inch pieces, or the German field and heavy guns. The General Staff's *Artillery in Offensive Operations* (quoted by Farndale for the 18-pdr's limit) would give them; it was not found.
96. [model/tables.md] Which 12-inch gun. BOH's "single 12-inch gun" at the First Army's disposal (p. 306) is taken to be a railway gun, the Mk IX's 32,700 yards. If it was another mounting the range changes.
97. [model/tables.md] The 2-inch and the 6-inch Newton mortar. The Canadian Corps' medium mortars are taken as 2-inch, as BOH's ammunition table names them (p. 315 n.2). The 6-inch Newton mortar was coming into service in 1917; whether any fired at Vimy is not known.
98. [model/tables.md] The 8-inch howitzers' marks at Vimy (Mk I-V conversions or Mk VI-VIII), which changes the range.
99. [model/tables.md] One rifle for both sides. The SMLE and the Gewehr 98 share the `rifle` id; their ranges differ (3,000 yards and 3,735 m) but their effect in the model is the same.
100. [model/tables.md] The 08/15's practical rate. No source read gives it; it is taken as the Lewis gun's 250 rounds a minute. A German training manual for the light machine gun would give it.

## Checklist

- [ ] Every estimate confirmed or corrected.
- [ ] Every leader rating confirmed or changed.
- [ ] Every disputed value settled or its range accepted.
- [ ] Open questions answered or accepted.

When every row is confirmed or corrected, change `status: draft` to `status: reviewed` in scenario.yaml.
